Romano assesses ‘perfect’ derby win, the restart and Milan’s mercato targets: “A busy January”

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Today, Fabrizio Romano has taken an in-depth look at AC Milan, their big weekend result and what’s next for the club.

After the Derby della Madonnina, there’s a very strong feeling surrounding Milan. All of a sudden, talk of winning a Scudetto under Ruben Amorim has gained genuine traction, and the group looks closer than ever.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

It’s not going to be easy, of course, but the Portuguese is obsessed with the idea of lifting the Serie A trophy with the Rossoneri, referencing as much in his press conference earlier today.

If that is going to happen this season, though, they will need to invest in January. After all, the squad remains shallow and extra support is very much needed.

Speaking to Time2Play.com, Romano looked at several things, including the derby and what the next few months could look like for the Diavolo.

“A derby can change the world. Because often, dynamics are triggered on and off the pitch, the atmosphere clears in an instant, and everyone contributes.

“AC Milan knows this well: a week ago, the home defeat against Bologna had brought much criticism, though it was also clearly the result of fatigue and significant absences, given the relentless pace of matches.

“But immediately afterwards, AC Milan set up their perfect derby—on and off the pitch, it’s no small matter—to build on and not just sit back.

“Because on the pitch, from Moreira to the usual Rabiot and Ruben Amorim, defended tooth and nail by Cardinale, everyone contributed that touch that led to the three points with a ferocious approach that became a winner.

“Off the pitch, Gerry Cardinale himself left his mark. Never like this.”

“The induction of legends during the week, Ricardo Kaká in the Hall of Fame, the announcement of the new stadium alongside Inter, interviews to staunchly defend Ruben Amorim, and above all, the desire to continue improving his AC Milan 2.0 starting… in January.

“Yes, because the Rossoneri’s post-derby signals are about building, not just about exaltation. This is where we need to start again, laying the foundations for a different, fierce, and winning Milan, like the one seen in the derby, which clearly can’t be enough.”

“And the first announcement will be that of the new Head of Football: Milan has a sealed verbal agreement with Markus Krösche, the man who has done a great job at Eintracht Frankfurt in recent years, both in scouting talent and capital gains, ready to take over the Rossoneri project.

“Krösche will be active for the January transfer window and will be in direct contact with Cardinale regarding the Rossoneri’s new plans.

“He’s a figure generating enthusiasm at Casa Milan, having already been the first choice since last June: Cardinale preferred to wait a few months to have his priority rather than fall back on managers who weren’t first choices. Now, the Krösche era is about to begin.”

“Milan’s objectives? It’s going to be a busy January: the defender could and should have arrived at the end of August, but there was no timeline or method. Milan has reinstated Tomori, but at least one defensive reinforcement is already planned for January.

“The favourite since June has been Sporting’s Gonçalo Inacio, but Milan has at least three other options on the list and has recently also sent Sylla of Union St. Gilloise to scout. It might not end there: keep an eye on the attack, where an additional option to give Gonçalo Ramos a rest shouldn’t be ruled out if the right opportunity presents itself.

“Assessments about Franceso Camarda will be made in the coming weeks, but Milan is exploring the transfer market and will continue to surprise. At least one signing, likely two. And then… the transfer market will dictate inspiration, ideas, and innovations.

“With new players and a Rossoneri that has never been so fired up in recent years.”

For the first time in a while, it’s a continued stream of positive news for Milan, which is only a good thing.

American Airlines Says AAdvantage Is Its Edge—These 10 Changes Would Make That True

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

American Airlines CEO Robert Isom calls AAdvantage one of the airline’s strongest competitive advantages. I agree. I still find more value in American’s program than in most of its competitors. Alaska can be better for single-partner awards, including domestic American Airlines flights. American itself can offer terrific deals on its own flights, alongside some truly terrible ones. One of AAdvantage’s best features is that the partner award chart hasn’t been devalued since 2016. But a good price for an award you can’t book doesn’t get you very far. American improved its Million Miler program in 2025, and has more changes coming there. But that’s really the only real improvement I can think of in the past few years. In the meantime, it eliminated fixed-price mileage upgrades and took seat selection and upgrade benefits away from elites buying Basic Economy. Some of my major complaints about the program: American offers paid upgrades to anyone on the flight while elite members wait and usually do not get upgraded. Systemwide upgrades are nearly impossible to use. Upgrades were once the major benefit of status but can no longer meaningfully be factored, of course dependent on route and dates of travel. When you aren’t upgraded, at least elites can confirm extra legroom seats – but American has far too few of those. And when you exclude exit row seats (not eligible with young children), I often have to pick my flights carefully because there just aren’t extra legroom seats available. American says they’re adding more – but won’t start until 2028. When they first introduced Main Cabin Extra they did the whole fleet in a matter of months! Scarce partner business class awards. British Airways guarantees award space for those redeeming Avios (whether their own, Finnair’s, Qatar’s, etc.) but those guarantee seats are unavailable for AAdvantage redemption. Qantas reserves premium cabin awards for its own members (they didn’t before the pandemic). Cathay Pacific does the same. Qatar limits access to its own premium cabin awards to those redeeming their points or those in other Avios programs. Etihad heavily limits redemptions by partners, to name just a few. These are all new restrictions in the past few years that make AAdvantage miles less valuable. Same day changes are highly restrictive. Elites can confirm changes to their bookings, but can’t change the routing – they can’t connect through a different hub than their original reservation. What’s the use of having the best domestic network when customers aren’t allowed to use it? And from many cities there aren’t multiple flights to a given hub, effectively eliminating the benefit entirely. Here are ten changes that would make earning American miles and status more worthwhile. Most have precedents at other airlines. Some are things American has already done, and could simply bring back. Give the best customers better award pricing. American doesn’t offer a standing award discount for its top elites or credit card customers. They used to have extra availability at the saver level for Executive Platinums – by phone only – but they eliminated this when dropping the idea of saver awards for their ow flights and going all-dynamic pricing. They also used to offer an award option of a 15% redemption rebate at 175,000 Loyalty Points but they dropped this for 2024. It only applied to one American Airlines award trip, for up to two passengers, with a claim process after travel. Bring it back, make the rebate automatic, and let members use it on the awards they actually book. An annual mileage cap would control the cost without requiring a narrowly defined roundtrip. American still offers award rebates at million-plus Loyalty Point milestones. Delta and United have elite or cardmember redemption discounts. Delta mostly seemed to just raise prices on everybody else, while United’s has actually opened up more saver awards and discounts even compared to the old saver level at times. American doesn’t have anything comparable, but should. In my ideal – I don’t expect American to budget for this – I’d love to see Executive Platinum and ConciergeKey members have a limited number of opportunities to open award space each year. British Airways Gold Guest List members have “jokers” that can release awards from specified paid inventory. It doesn’t have to mean last seat avialability on every flight. It could be capacity controlled with a certain level of revenue inventory. Make partner awards easier to get, including when schedules first open American’s partner chart is very good (10 years ago the devaluation was brutal, but many airlines have devalued more since then). The challenge now is access to partner award space, with so many partners withholding inventory from American. British Airways shares revenue with American in a transatlantic joint venture that’s supposed to make them indifferent about whose aircraft a passenger flies. Yet Avios programs even outside the joint venture can get British Airways award space that American can’t. Cathay Pacific, Qantas, Qatar Airways and Etihad also give their own members or selected partners access that AAdvantage doesn’t get. American should negotiate for access, pay for additional inventory, and make reciprocal access part of its partnership discussions. Start with British Airways, despite draconian fuel surcharges that some other partners don’t even charge, simply because their close commercial relationship should produce a better result for customers – otherwise why are we allowing antitrust immunity in the first place? And keep the existing partner chart, don’t use this better inventory as an excuse to reprice the seats. American should also stop making members wait for its own booking calendar to open. American books up to 331 days ahead, while Finnair opens awards 360 days out. When a partner releases seats earlier, AAdvantage members should be able to book them then. Nearly a month’s head start matters when there are only a couple of business class awards. Many years ago that was the practice at Alaska Airlines – you could book the partner award right away, and call up later to add Alaska connecting flights when their own scheduled loaded. Give top elites priority for an empty middle seatI argued for this in 2020. It would be even more useful now, with complimentary upgrades harder to count on and not enough Main Cabin Extra seats. When a Platinum Pro, Executive Platinum or ConciergeKey member sits in a window or aisle seat in regular coach, hold the adjacent middle seat out from seat assignments. Have AgentAssist automatically release it at the gate when needed. The airline would keep selling the flight. It wouldn’t turn away a paying passenger to preserve an empty seat. Families who need to sit together and operational requirements could take priority. But when some middle seats are going to wind upempty anyway, ensuring the empty seat is beside the airline’s best customers simply makes sense. American could deliver a meaningful comfort benefit with very little displaced revenue. Make confirmed upgrades from paid premium economy a permanent option American has already tested this twice. In March and again in September, it offered promotions where a member could buy premium economy and apply a systemwide upgrade to confirm business class without the usual upgrade inventory. Make that a regular feature of systemwide upgrades: buy premium economy, spend a certificate, and confirm business class when a seat is still for sale. If American needs a short, published list of blackout dates, that’s fair enough. The existing ability to upgrade from coach should stay. This would give members an additional way to use their certificates, with certainty in exchange for buying a more expensive ticket. Give top elites 20% off paid upgrades, and let them earn upgrade creditsAmerican sells seats that once would have gone to complimentary upgrades. They value the tens of dollars more than rewarding the customer who spends tens of thousands. But upgrades were the key reason to earn status, which is the key driver of cobrand card spend. Give Platinum Pro and higher members 20% off the paid upgrade offers they see. American already has AA20 discounts for employees and eligible family members on airfare and awards. A comparable discount on upgrade offers for top customers isn’t an out of sample idea. The price before the discount needs to be the same offer the customer would otherwise receive. And American might collect more money from members who currently decline a buy-up because they’re waiting for a free upgrade. Some existing buyers would pay less while some people who never buy would start. Elites would get a better deal on excess premium inventory than once a year flyers. Add upgrade credits at Loyalty Point Reward milestones, usable toward these offers. That would provide something useful even when a systemwide upgrade won’t clear. Let top elites use the route network for same-day changes American has a huge network. Its same-day change rules prevent members from making full use of it. Under American’s rules, a replacement itinerary needs the same airports and routing. If you’re connecting through Charlotte, an earlier option through Dallas doesn’t necessarily help you. Neither does a nonstop replacing your connection. I was writing about this problem in 2015. The same-routing restriction already existed then, though agents had often been willing to waive it. Let Platinum Pro and higher members confirm a same-day change between the same origin and destination using any reasonable American routing with seats available in the cabin they’re entitled to occupy. Keep it to the day of travel, so this doesn’t become a way to buy a cheaper itinerary weeks ahead and immediately swap into an expensive one. Let an already-purchased upgrade carry over when the new flight has room in that cabin. A meeting ends early, the member gets home sooner, and American gives a frequent traveler a real benefit for their loyalty. Bring back stopovers on awards. Alaska allows free stopovers on one-way awards. Air France KLM’s Flying Blue offers them, too. Aeroplan charges just 5,000 additional points for an eligible stopover. American should let members stop at a connecting city on an international award that prices based on the partner redemption chart. Spend several days in Tokyo on the way to somewhere else in Asia, or visit Helsinki, London or Madrid before continuing through Europe. This should be free for top elites, but consider charging 5,000 miles for everyone else. Count award flights toward status and Million Miler balancesAmerican encourages members to earn miles through credit cards, shopping, hotels and other partners. When they finally redeem those miles and fly, the award trip doesn’t earn Loyalty Points. Publish a simple rate of Loyalty Points per mile redeemed. Include partner flights booked with AAdvantage miles. American issued the ticket and knows what the member redeemed. It could begin with its own flights if that’s easier to administer, but that shouldn’t be a permanent limit. Actual distance flown on awards should count toward Million Miler status as well. Delta already counts award travel toward its lifetime totals. Let elite households pool miles for free and share benefits easilyA family can collectively have enough miles for an award while each individual account comes up short. American should let an elite member establish a household pool, with no charge for combining miles. LifeMiles makes family pooling an elite benefit. Other programs manage pooling, too. Require verified members, put limits on how often people can join or leave, and give the household head redemption authority. Restricting the benefit to elites should make some forms of abuse less attractive, though it wouldn’t eliminate fraud. But the optimal amount of fraud is not zero anyway. American already lets members give someone status for a day. Make that benefit work like a trip benefit: select the traveler and reservation, then have the eligible seating and airport benefits apply immediately. The current terms require selecting the usage day at least seven days ahead Give Million Milers a benefit every yearAmerican deserves credit for adding lifetime Platinum Pro at four million miles and Executive Platinum at five million. It has also announced more choices for Million Miler milestone rewards. The next improvement should be something that arrives every year, including years when the member doesn’t cross another million mile threshold. Alaska gives Million Milers an annual head start toward status, which keeps them flying rather than retiring from engagement after they hit their threshold. American could also let a Million Miler share status with a designated household member, as competing programs do, or choose an annual benefit such as upgrade credit. Delta’s higher lifetime tiers bring recurring Choice Benefits. I’d start with the annual Loyalty Points. Someone who has already flown American a million miles shouldn’t have to begin every year’s pursuit of higher status at the same zero as someone who just joined. What do you feel American Airlines AAdvantage is missing, and how does it compare to other programs today? I see only Alaska Airlines Atmos Rewards as comparable among U.S. airlines, believing both American and Alaska’s programs to be better than United’s. Some of you will disagree! And Alaska’s has its own challenges, including inventory for award redemption on its own flights and combining different partners on the same award ticket. Which of these changes I’ve suggested would move the needle for your own loyalty? And what am I missing from this list? Here are ten changes that would make earning American miles and status more worthwhile. Most have precedents at other airlines. Some are things American has already done, and could simply bring back.

Give the best customers better award pricing. American doesn’t offer a standing award discount for its top elites or credit card customers. They used to have extra availability at the saver level for Executive Platinums – by phone only – but they eliminated this when dropping the idea of saver awards for their ow flights and going all-dynamic pricing.

They also used to offer an award option of a 15% redemption rebate at 175,000 Loyalty Points but they dropped this for 2024. It only applied to one American Airlines award trip, for up to two passengers, with a claim process after travel. Bring it back, make the rebate automatic, and let members use it on the awards they actually book. An annual mileage cap would control the cost without requiring a narrowly defined roundtrip. American still offers award rebates at million-plus Loyalty Point milestones.

Delta and United have elite or cardmember redemption discounts. Delta mostly seemed to just raise prices on everybody else, while United’s has actually opened up more saver awards and discounts even compared to the old saver level at times. American doesn’t have anything comparable, but should.

In my ideal – I don’t expect American to budget for this – I’d love to see Executive Platinum and ConciergeKey members have a limited number of opportunities to open award space each year. British Airways Gold Guest List members have “jokers” that can release awards from specified paid inventory. It doesn’t have to mean last seat avialability on every flight. It could be capacity controlled with a certain level of revenue inventory.

British Airways shares revenue with American in a transatlantic joint venture that’s supposed to make them indifferent about whose aircraft a passenger flies. Yet Avios programs even outside the joint venture can get British Airways award space that American can’t. Cathay Pacific, Qantas, Qatar Airways and Etihad also give their own members or selected partners access that AAdvantage doesn’t get. American should negotiate for access, pay for additional inventory, and make reciprocal access part of its partnership discussions. Start with British Airways, despite draconian fuel surcharges that some other partners don’t even charge, simply because their close commercial relationship should produce a better result for customers – otherwise why are we allowing antitrust immunity in the first place? And keep the existing partner chart, don’t use this better inventory as an excuse to reprice the seats. American should also stop making members wait for its own booking calendar to open. American books up to 331 days ahead, while Finnair opens awards 360 days out. When a partner releases seats earlier, AAdvantage members should be able to book them then. Nearly a month’s head start matters when there are only a couple of business class awards. Many years ago that was the practice at Alaska Airlines – you could book the partner award right away, and call up later to add Alaska connecting flights when their own scheduled loaded. Give top elites priority for an empty middle seatI argued for this in 2020. It would be even more useful now, with complimentary upgrades harder to count on and not enough Main Cabin Extra seats. When a Platinum Pro, Executive Platinum or ConciergeKey member sits in a window or aisle seat in regular coach, hold the adjacent middle seat out from seat assignments. Have AgentAssist automatically release it at the gate when needed. The airline would keep selling the flight. It wouldn’t turn away a paying passenger to preserve an empty seat. Families who need to sit together and operational requirements could take priority. But when some middle seats are going to wind upempty anyway, ensuring the empty seat is beside the airline’s best customers simply makes sense. American could deliver a meaningful comfort benefit with very little displaced revenue. Make confirmed upgrades from paid premium economy a permanent option American has already tested this twice. In March and again in September, it offered promotions where a member could buy premium economy and apply a systemwide upgrade to confirm business class without the usual upgrade inventory. Make that a regular feature of systemwide upgrades: buy premium economy, spend a certificate, and confirm business class when a seat is still for sale. If American needs a short, published list of blackout dates, that’s fair enough. The existing ability to upgrade from coach should stay. This would give members an additional way to use their certificates, with certainty in exchange for buying a more expensive ticket. Give top elites 20% off paid upgrades, and let them earn upgrade creditsAmerican sells seats that once would have gone to complimentary upgrades. They value the tens of dollars more than rewarding the customer who spends tens of thousands. But upgrades were the key reason to earn status, which is the key driver of cobrand card spend. Give Platinum Pro and higher members 20% off the paid upgrade offers they see. American already has AA20 discounts for employees and eligible family members on airfare and awards. A comparable discount on upgrade offers for top customers isn’t an out of sample idea. The price before the discount needs to be the same offer the customer would otherwise receive. And American might collect more money from members who currently decline a buy-up because they’re waiting for a free upgrade. Some existing buyers would pay less while some people who never buy would start. Elites would get a better deal on excess premium inventory than once a year flyers. Add upgrade credits at Loyalty Point Reward milestones, usable toward these offers. That would provide something useful even when a systemwide upgrade won’t clear. Let top elites use the route network for same-day changes American has a huge network. Its same-day change rules prevent members from making full use of it. Under American’s rules, a replacement itinerary needs the same airports and routing. If you’re connecting through Charlotte, an earlier option through Dallas doesn’t necessarily help you. Neither does a nonstop replacing your connection. I was writing about this problem in 2015. The same-routing restriction already existed then, though agents had often been willing to waive it. Let Platinum Pro and higher members confirm a same-day change between the same origin and destination using any reasonable American routing with seats available in the cabin they’re entitled to occupy. Keep it to the day of travel, so this doesn’t become a way to buy a cheaper itinerary weeks ahead and immediately swap into an expensive one. Let an already-purchased upgrade carry over when the new flight has room in that cabin. A meeting ends early, the member gets home sooner, and American gives a frequent traveler a real benefit for their loyalty. Bring back stopovers on awards. Alaska allows free stopovers on one-way awards. Air France KLM’s Flying Blue offers them, too. Aeroplan charges just 5,000 additional points for an eligible stopover. American should let members stop at a connecting city on an international award that prices based on the partner redemption chart. Spend several days in Tokyo on the way to somewhere else in Asia, or visit Helsinki, London or Madrid before continuing through Europe. This should be free for top elites, but consider charging 5,000 miles for everyone else. Count award flights toward status and Million Miler balancesAmerican encourages members to earn miles through credit cards, shopping, hotels and other partners. When they finally redeem those miles and fly, the award trip doesn’t earn Loyalty Points. Publish a simple rate of Loyalty Points per mile redeemed. Include partner flights booked with AAdvantage miles. American issued the ticket and knows what the member redeemed. It could begin with its own flights if that’s easier to administer, but that shouldn’t be a permanent limit. Actual distance flown on awards should count toward Million Miler status as well. Delta already counts award travel toward its lifetime totals. Let elite households pool miles for free and share benefits easilyA family can collectively have enough miles for an award while each individual account comes up short. American should let an elite member establish a household pool, with no charge for combining miles. LifeMiles makes family pooling an elite benefit. Other programs manage pooling, too. Require verified members, put limits on how often people can join or leave, and give the household head redemption authority. Restricting the benefit to elites should make some forms of abuse less attractive, though it wouldn’t eliminate fraud. But the optimal amount of fraud is not zero anyway. American already lets members give someone status for a day. Make that benefit work like a trip benefit: select the traveler and reservation, then have the eligible seating and airport benefits apply immediately. The current terms require selecting the usage day at least seven days ahead Give Million Milers a benefit every yearAmerican deserves credit for adding lifetime Platinum Pro at four million miles and Executive Platinum at five million. It has also announced more choices for Million Miler milestone rewards. The next improvement should be something that arrives every year, including years when the member doesn’t cross another million mile threshold. Alaska gives Million Milers an annual head start toward status, which keeps them flying rather than retiring from engagement after they hit their threshold. American could also let a Million Miler share status with a designated household member, as competing programs do, or choose an annual benefit such as upgrade credit. Delta’s higher lifetime tiers bring recurring Choice Benefits. I’d start with the annual Loyalty Points. Someone who has already flown American a million miles shouldn’t have to begin every year’s pursuit of higher status at the same zero as someone who just joined. What do you feel American Airlines AAdvantage is missing, and how does it compare to other programs today? I see only Alaska Airlines Atmos Rewards as comparable among U.S. airlines, believing both American and Alaska’s programs to be better than United’s. Some of you will disagree! And Alaska’s has its own challenges, including inventory for award redemption on its own flights and combining different partners on the same award ticket. Which of these changes I’ve suggested would move the needle for your own loyalty? And what am I missing from this list? Gary Leff is one of the foremost experts in the field of miles, points, and frequent business travel - a topic he has covered since 2002. Co-founder of frequent flyer community InsideFlyer.com, emcee of the Freddie Awards, and named one of the "World's Top Travel Experts" by Conde' Nast Traveler (2010-Present) Gary has been a guest on most major news media, profiled in several top print publications, and published broadly on the topic of consumer loyalty. Excellent article, and I agree with most of it. However, you are leaving out one major miss in AA’s Million Miler program. The highest level, CK, is unachievable, unlike all their domestic competitors. Until they change that, their Million Miler program simply isn’t competitive. If you can’t chase the top tier, you’re chasing mediocrity (especially on AA today).

Excellent list. They need more MCE on their planes – it’s a competitive disadvantage compared to UA and DL. It’s the main perk of elite status these days. Software to auto-upgrade you to an MCE seat only works if there are, you know, more MCE seats.

Other than that, while most of this list would be nice to have, #1 on your list (discount award tickets for elites and cardholders) is also a need to have. It’s a competitive disadvantage compared to UA in a meaningful way, and DL in a still meaningful but somewhat more theoretical way given their often sky high redemption rates.

Bravo! Making the most of your strengths makes more sense than struggling with improving your weaknesses. I agree with all of these proposals especially better SWU options, million miler accrual and loyalty points for award tickets, and special perks for Platinum Pro and above. I’d be surprised if American adopts any of them, however.

In the business of monetizing elite programs (ie. liquidating the goodwill assets for short term gain). Typical hustles: (1) Hey, the other airline is worse. (2) Our downgrades are actually improvements if you look at it right.

Given how hard it is to find premium award inventory in general, I wish they would make it easier to combine award segments with paid segments. I’m sometimes willing to buy a ticket DCA to CLT so I can then use the business class award from CLT to LHR, for example. But the airline doesn’t see both segments as part of one itinerary and doesn’t protect me if my first segment takes a delay and I misconnect. They also won’t let me check my bags all the way through. Ripple CTO David Schwartz Explains Why XRP Could Gain Value as XRPL Adoption Grows

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ripple CTO emeritus David Schwartz has argued that rising adoption of the XRP Ledger (XRPL) could translate into stronger demand for XRP, pointing to the token’s role in liquidity, trading, and network transactions.

In an October 23 exchange on X, Schwartz challenged the idea that the ledger could become a major financial platform without its native asset gaining meaningful demand.

“The thesis is this: Low fees and reliable operation make XRPL a great place to move and hold value. That drives value flow to XRPL. XRP captures some of that value because of its unique advantages over other assets on XRPL,” Schwartz wrote.

He further noted that XRP has several advantages that could help it benefit as more financial activity moves onto the network. These include its role as a liquidity bridge between assets, its availability to accounts without counterparty risk, and its use to pay transaction fees.

“I think that’s one example of the mechanism I’m talking about. XRP has other advantages on XRPL too. It’s the only counterparty-free asset that any account can accept. People have to hold it to pay fees. All of those things can help drive value and demand,” he added.

Notably, the ledger’s built-in decentralized exchange can route trades through XRP when doing so offers a better exchange rate than trading two assets directly. If XRPL supports more markets and tokenized assets, XRP could therefore see greater use in connecting liquidity across those markets.

Schwartz said a scenario in which XRPL becomes highly successful in financial applications while XRP fails to attract strong demand is “very unlikely.” He acknowledged that a world dominated by a single settlement asset could produce a different outcome, but said he considers that possibility unlikely.

He also gave a hypothetical example involving Bitcoin. According to him, even if Bitcoin became the dominant settlement asset, wrapped or bridged versions of BTC could still trade on XRPL, creating opportunities for XRP to facilitate exchanges.

That said, on-chain data also offer another measure of adoption. Santiment reported last Thursday that the XRP Ledger had reached a record 8.24 million non-empty wallets, while daily wallet creation climbed to 4,772, its fifth-largest spike since March. This suggests that participation on XRPL has continued despite price volatility, which could generate sustained upward pressure on XRP.

Moreover, the firm identified $1.40 as an important level to watch, with a sustained move above it potentially opening the way toward $1.50 if wallet growth remains strong. Conversely, it warned that a drop below $1.30 alongside slower wallet creation would weaken the short-term adoption signal.

Some analysts have offered substantially more bullish price projections. Recently, Egrag Crypto said XRP appeared positioned to extend toward a macro target above $15, citing a chart pattern based on previous periods of price compression and breakout.

At press time, XRP was trading at $1.31, up 2.91% in the past 24 hours.

The 2027 BMW 740 xDrive Builds on Excellence

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Revised design and an updated interior sharpen the case for BMW’s biggest executive sedan.

BMW’s 7-series has been in a race with the Mercedes-Benz S-class for nearly 50 years over the German executive sedan crown. But an avant-garde, new-generation reimagining in 2022 knocked the long-wheelbase sedan off its pedestal. Four years later, BMW has tweaked the 7-series’s front and rear design and upgraded the interior with better technology. Also, it’s lost the “i” from the end of its badge, as part of BMW’s new policy on model naming. Can a shorter name, a prettier face, and new screens push it back into contention?

Our expert editors test every vehicle we review. Read more about how we test and review cars here.

The 2027 BMW 740 xDrive’s most significant change is apparent at first glance. Horizontal grille slats inside the sizable kidney grille replace the previous vertical ones. Split headlights stick around, with a new vertical, indented integration below the daytime running lights. Extended LED taillights are also new for 2027. Inside, the BMW 740 receives much of the same treatment that’s been given to the Neue Klasse EV models, including a new four-spoke steering wheel, a snappier operating system, and a sleek panoramic display.

Engine: turbocharged and mild-hybridized 2998-cc inline-six

Transmission: 8-speed automatic, all-wheel drive

In the hills above Lake Elsinore, the 740 xDrive showed off just how magically BMW’s adaptive chassis control system is, especially in its more able Professional guise. This uses full air suspension and active roll stabilization through an electrical system that applies torque against the sway bars to counter lean under cornering loads. So equipped, the 7-series rewarded a spirited pace on California canyon roads, despite a 4800-pound curb weight sitting atop a 126.6-inch wheelbase.

Front-end turn-in was sharp, and the minuscule amount of body roll effectively hid the magnitude of weight swinging around. Like many recent BMWs, the steering was nicely weighted but largely mute, forcing dynamic awareness to come from the base of the seat instead of through my fingers. While plentiful electronically ordered handling adjustments were no doubt happening in real time, these intrusions felt imperceptible behind the wheel.

But the 740 isn’t a one-trick pony, either. Out on the highway, the 7-series serves a different customer. The same roll stabilization technology that keeps it planted in the canyons can decouple the roll bars for straight-line driving, reducing unwanted movements in the cabin and allowing the air suspension to soften even more. The result was a ride that felt effortlessly smooth over freeway joints but never turned wallowing. Luxury vehicles shouldn’t translate bumps and dips inside the cabin, and the 740 expertly balances stability at the wheel with a supple experience in the rear seat. Cruising at California highway speeds was where the 7-series felt truly at home.

Similarly, the turbocharged and electrified 3.0-liter inline-six engine is powerful and smooth, but certainly not shouty. With 394 hp, the 740 has the same power as a base-model X5, but it didn’t feel lacking at the helm. The 398-lb-ft torque peak comes in at 1850 rpm, which pushed the big sedan along gracefully without apparent effort. But winding the engine to the 5200 rpm at which peak power arrives elicited a pleasant, if speaker-amplified, song—although it felt largely unnecessary given the muscle of the torque curve.

Paired with the ever-amicable ZF eight-speed automatic transmission, the powertrain of the 740 felt like a means to an end rather than a especial highlight, but that’s how most buyers of large luxury sedans will expect it to be. Sure, you can paddle-shift through the gears and enjoy the gearbox’s satisfyingly snappy responses, but how many owners are likely to regularly do so?

BMW is proud of its hands-free Highway Assistant semiautonomous driving system, and so I put this to the test on I-5 South. The SAE Level 2 system can drive hands-free up to 85 mph as long as you keep a forward gaze, and it did so well. From slowing for construction zones to autonomous lane changes, the semiautonomous system handled busy California traffic smartly and smoothly.

My only real complaint with the updated 7-series was its soft brake pedal. Even with the sizable standard rotors, the soft pedal tuning made the heavy sedan feel underbraked, requiring more force than I was expecting to need to apply for a quick stop. Plus, like most of the R&T team who have encountered it, I don’t like the new four-spoke steering wheel with its strange disconnected spokes and awkward haptic touch controls.

With a mild facelift and the same powertrains as before, the 2027 7-series’s most noticeable upgrades are all inside. BMW uses many buzzwords and acronyms to describe the new interior experience, but the most important one is the Panoramic iDrive system. As in the X5, iX3, i3, and new 3-series, the Neue Klasse direction has removed the old physical iDrive controller and replaced it with a fully touchscreen system. The 7-series now has a 17.9-inch central screen, a recessed 14.6-inch passenger display, and the long panoramic gauge cluster that sits atop the dashboard.

In practice, the new user experience was a mixed bag. I’m quick to criticize any vehicle that lacks tactile controls, and the glamour of BMW’s new system doesn’t belie this fault. Save for a scrolling volume control near the nub shifter, everything from climate control to vehicle settings must be manipulated through the central touchscreen. The screen responds quickly and has a permanent menu with obvious icons for climate control, vehicle settings, navigation, and media inputs. However, tapping on any of these icons once is only the start of a multistep process, much of which will be done with eyes off the road.

To adjust the suspension or ride height on the 740, I had to click through four menus before the change was complete. Even the seat’s lumbar support has to be adjusted through the touchscreen. That’s a bridge too far in my book.

Despite these annoyances, BMW’s new system mostly excels at providing driving information with the projected gauge cluster. Illuminated on a span of masked glass across the dashboard, this instrument display was perfectly placed and kept my field of vision clear. BMW’s projection direction is smartly tuned, with speed placed directly in front of the driver and navigation to the right. Interesting but superfluous information like elevation is projected nearer to the passenger side. My only real complaint was a hard-to-decipher tachometer.

My Frozen Purple 740 xDrive was well appointed with white Merino leather seats that were heated, cooled, and massaging. The seating experience was unimpeachably comfortable except for the loud fan noise while using the ventilation function. Seating adjustment is now done through door-mounted buttons, allowing BMW to make the seats even wider. The equipped Dolby Atmos system was predictably excellent, and interior sound levels were nearly silent even without active tunes.

As brands like Audi and Lexus move away from the ultraluxury sedan market, BMW’s 7-series is left with a single natural competitor—the Mercedes-Benz S-class. But the fight isn’t exactly equal, either. The 740 starts at $101,350 in a rear-wheel-drive configuration, while the most affordable S-class starts at $114,100. Mercedes offers more horsepower and torque for the money, but the luxury-sedan buyer is unlikely to be motivated exclusively by performance.

Of course, if they are, then the 740 sits at the base of the range, with both the hybrid 750e and twin-turbo V-8 M760 positioned above it. Plus, of course, the continued novelty of the fully electric i7 EV, which shares the same bodywork.

Far be it from me to pretend reviewing a BMW 7-series is hard work, but the massaging seat feature certainly helped me calm down after hours of driving. BMW offers a variety of intensity settings and location-focused massages, meaning you can tailor the experience to where you need it most. It isn’t too hard or unnatural-feeling, and it still provides genuine relief.

Audi Digital Matrix LED: Adaptive Beams Are Here

A cosmic pond

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Massimo Tamajo from Syracuse, Sicily, Italy

IC 410 is an emission nebula about 12,000 light-years away in Auriga, lit by the radiation from the embedded star cluster NGC 1893. The region is best known as home to the Tadpoles — two pillars of gas and dust that are forming stars. The imager took about 2.1 hours of exposure with a 3.5-inch f/6.8 astrograph, one-shot color camera, and Hα/OIII dual-band filter, processed in an HOO palette.

5 Gbps service sits above the company’s 2 Gbps, 1 Gbps, and 300 Mbps fiber plans

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Just over a year ago, T-Mobile launched its home fiber internet service in the United States, buoyed by its acquisition of Lumos. At the time, T-Mobile’s fiber service reached half a million customers across the country, with speeds of up to 2 Gbps. This week, T-Mobile introduced a new 5 Gbps tier for customers who crave even more speed.

The 5 Gbps tier is priced at $90 per month and comes with a 10-year price guarantee. Given how often legacy cable internet providers would raise prices, forcing customers to jump through hoops to keep the status quo, a 10-year price lock could be very appealing to a large segment of the population. But as with most things, there is some fine print to the offer, which reads, “Taxes & fees, voluntary equipment or speed upgrades, select limited-time promotions, per-use charges, third-party services, and network management practices aren’t included.”

To get the $90/month price, you’ll also need to set up AutoPay with T-Mobile. However, to further sweeten the deal, customers will also receive a mesh Wi-Fi extender that broadens the coverage of the included Fiber gateway.

For new customers, T-Mobile is also offering two months of free service (via bill credits) and a $300 virtual prepaid debit card. T-Mobile’s pricing for the 5 Gbps plan is an aggressive deal in the home fiber market. For example, GFiber (Google) costs $100/month for 3 Gbps speeds, while the company’s 8 Gbps tier is $150/month.

T-Mobile’s new 5 Gbps plan joins the existing 2 Gbps ($70/month), 1 Gbps ($60/month), and 300 Mbps ($45/month) plans. All of the plans feature symmetrical upload/download speeds, which is a big upgrade for anyone coming from a traditional cable internet provider.

For people who don’t have access to T-Mobile’s growing fiber footprint in the U.S., the company also offers its unlimited 5G Home Internet service, with prices starting at $50/month.

Eintracht Frankfurt prepared to renew Arnaud Kalimuendo pursuit

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Eintracht Frankfurt are reportedly preparing another attempt to bring Nottingham Forest striker Arnaud Kalimuendo back to the Bundesliga during the coming January 2027 transfer window. According to Absolutfussball, the 24-year-old Frenchman remains the Hessen club’s preferred attacking reinforcement.

Frankfurt have reportedly identified two positions requiring reinforcement in January. In addition to a centre-forward, the club intend to strengthen their fullback options. The former PSG and Rennes striker spent the second half of last season on loan at Frankfurt, scoring six goals in 19 Bundesliga appearances.

His performances prompted Eintracht to consider a permanent transfer, but the club ultimately declined to exercise a purchase option reportedly worth €27m.

The latest developments represent another chapter in Frankfurt’s prolonged efforts to retain the French attacker. As previously reported in a Get German Football News August transfer update, Eintracht pursued a second loan agreement with Nottingham Forest during the final days of the summer transfer window.

Negotiations nevertheless proved complicated. Forest reportedly demanded a transfer fee exceeding €30m, a figure that would have represented a club-record investment for Frankfurt. The Premier League outfit were also understood to prefer a permanent arrangement rather than another temporary departure.

Eintracht eventually abandoned their deadline-day pursuit, concentrating instead on completing outgoing transfers. Kalimuendo remained in England, where his prospects of regular first-team football have subsequently deteriorated.

The Frenchman has been restricted to brief appearances for Nottingham Forest this season, failing to register a goal contribution. His lack of playing time could provide Frankfurt with renewed leverage in negotiations, particularly if Forest become more receptive to another loan arrangement.

Whether the Premier League club would consider lowering its previous asking price remains unclear. No concrete financial details concerning Frankfurt’s proposed January approach have yet emerged.

The renewed interest is particularly noteworthy given the performances of Frankfurt’s existing centre-forwards. Jonathan Burkardt and Younes Ebnoutalib have both enjoyed productive starts to the 2026/27 campaign. The pair have continued to supply goals under Hütter, while the Austrian has increasingly emphasized the importance of managing his attacking options according to the tactical requirements of individual matches.

This development contrasts with the circumstances surrounding Kalimuendo’s original departure. As examined in GGFN’s extensive Frankfurt preseason tactical preview, the club initially appeared capable of proceeding without another expensive attacking acquisition. Burkardt had established himself as a reliable central option, while Ebnoutalib was emerging as a genuine alternative.

Both players have subsequently strengthened their respective claims. Kalimuendo would therefore arrive not as an automatic solution to an underperforming attack, but as another established Bundesliga option capable of increasing competition and providing Hütter with greater tactical flexibility.

The Frenchman’s mobility, ability to attack spaces behind opposing defensive lines, and willingness to contribute without possession would also make him an intriguing fit within Hütter’s preferred attacking approach.

Frankfurt’s wider financial and sporting circumstances add another consideration. Following Markus Krösche’s departure, the club are undergoing a significant restructuring of their sporting leadership, with a greater emphasis on financial sustainability and carefully targeted recruitment.

Kalimuendo’s previous Frankfurt spell demonstrated that he can contribute in the Bundesliga. His limited playing time at Nottingham Forest may now create more favourable negotiating conditions than those encountered during the summer.

Whether those circumstances finally allow Eintracht to secure the return of a player they have pursued for months remains an open question.

Riot Liquidates 79% of Its Bitcoin Treasury for $28 Billion AI Infrastructure Deal

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

American Riot Platforms Inc. (RIOT) sold off 79% of its Bitcoin in the third quarter of 2026, redirecting capital toward building infrastructure for artificial intelligence. According to a fresh financial report, the company reduced its cryptocurrency reserves from 11,380 to 2,418 BTC.

In total, over the first nine months of the year, Riot sold 20,264 BTC on the market, receiving about $1.5 billion for them. This money went toward financing a major technological shift: the company's free energy capacity is being repurposed for high-performance computing needs.

Riot shares reacted to the session open with a sharp jump to $16.20, after which they stabilized in the $15.45–$15.50 range, holding positions on elevated volumes.

The main incentive for selling the cryptocurrency was a 15-year agreement to lease ready-made sites and capacities (powered land lease) totaling 910 MW at the Corsicana campus in Texas. The strategic partner was the Frontier AI Lab (Anthropic) laboratory.

The initial cost of the contract is estimated at $28 billion, and the average annual rent at the start will be about $1.8 billion. Taking into account other projects, Riot's total long-term revenue in the data center segment has reached $38 billion, and the total controlled capacity is 1,151 MW.

Riot CEO Jason Les noted that the agreement is structured as a triple-net lease. Under its terms, the client itself purchases and maintains the computing equipment. For Riot, this reduces construction costs by roughly half compared to standard Tier 3 data centers. As a result, the company will be able to do without issuing new shares and diluting investors' stakes.

The shift in focus is occurring against the backdrop of falling margins in classic mining. Riot's quarterly revenue from cryptocurrency mining fell by 28% — to $116 million, although the company actually mined 1,637 BTC.

Nevertheless, total quarterly revenue held at $181.4 million (+1% year-over-year), outpacing market forecasts. Net profit amounted to $4.03 million.

To optimize its balance sheet, the company fully closed its $200 million credit line with Coinbase, returning 5,821 BTC from collateral. Riot ended the quarter with free liquidity of $950.2 million (including cash and the remainder of the cryptocurrency). The balance sheet retains 2,418 BTC, which may also go under the hammer in the fourth quarter to finance the Texas construction.

Court to hear argument on whether and when federal officials can be held liable for violations of the Constitution

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court will hear oral argument on Monday, Nov. 9, in a lawsuit brought by a Hawaii man, Kekai Watanabe, who contends that a prison paramedic violated his constitutional rights when he failed to provide medical treatment after Watanabe was injured during a fight while in federal custody. Both the paramedic, Francis Nielsen, and the Trump administration, which filed a “friend of the court” brief supporting Nielsen, urge the justices to shut down Watanabe’s case; Nielsen also suggests that the court should go even further and foreclose efforts to recover from federal officials for violations of the Constitution.

More than 50 years ago, in Bivens v. Six Unknown Named Agents of Federal Bureau of Narcotics, the Supreme Court ruled that a violation of an individual’s constitutional rights by a federal official can create a federal claim for damages.

In the years that followed, the justices have extended their decision in Bivens only twice. In Davis v. Passman, in 1979, the court held that a former congressional staffer could sue a member of Congress for alleged sex discrimination in violation of the Fifth Amendment. And in Carlson v. Green, the court held that the estate of an inmate, who died after having an asthma attack, could bring a Bivens claim when prison officials had failed to provide adequate medical care for eight hours after the attack and did not send him to the hospital during that time.

But since then the court has resisted efforts to rely on Bivens. Four years ago, in Egbert v. Boule, it observed that it had “declined 11 times to imply a similar cause of action for other alleged constitutional violations” and that “in all but the most unusual circumstances, prescribing a cause of action is a job for Congress, not the courts.” Moreover, Justice Clarence Thomas noted for the majority “that if we were called to decide Bivens today, we would decline to discover any implied causes of action in the Constitution. But,” Thomas wrote, “to decide the case before us, we need not reconsider Bivens itself.”

In the present case, Watanabe was badly injured during a gang-related fight while at a federal detention center in Hawaii. He contends that he told Nielsen, who treated him, that his pain was a 10 on a scale of 1 to 10, but Nielsen gave him only over-the-counter pain medication. Watanabe alleges that he was kept in solitary confinement for more than two months, during which he did not see a specialist or go to a hospital. When Watanabe eventually obtained an x-ray seven months later, it revealed that he had a fractured coccyx and that chips from the bone had penetrated the soft tissue in the area. He says that he continues to be in “tremendous pain.”

In February 2022, Watanabe went to federal court in Honolulu. Relying on Bivens and Carlson, he alleged that Nielsen violated his rights under the Eighth Amendment to be free of cruel and unusual punishment because Nielsen was deliberately indifferent to his serious medical needs.

The district court dismissed Watanabe’s claim. It held that Carlson did not apply because his claim arose in a new context – that is, it was not sufficiently similar to the facts of that case for his claim to go forward.

The U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit reversed. It acknowledged that the Supreme Court “has cautioned against extending Bivens remedies to new contexts,” but in its view “Watanabe’s claim is identical to Carlson in all meaningful respects” and therefore can proceed. A deeply divided court of appeals declined to rehear the case.

Nielsen appealed to the Supreme Court, which agreed to hear the case in June.

In his brief on the merits, Nielsen contends that Watanabe’s case fails both prongs of the “demanding two-part test” that the Supreme Court has established for Bivens claims. That test, he adds, boils down to “whether there is any reason to think Congress might be better equipped to create a damages remedy.”

The first question is whether the claim arises in a new context from the three cases in which it has recognized Bivens claims. “Even ‘small’ differences from the original Bivens trio” of cases “will present a new context,” Nielsen writes: “‘a modest extension is still an extension.’” Watanabe’s claim, Nielsen argues, is not modest but is instead “meaningfully—profoundly—different” from Carlson. As an initial matter, he says, the facts are markedly dissimilar, because the inmate in Carlson died just hours after the attack, so that monetary compensation for his estate was the only possible remedy. By contrast, Nielsen asserts, Watanabe’s “injury was neither fatal nor life-threatening,” and instead of monetary damages he could have sought more treatment or a move to a hospital through either internal prison appeals or by filing a lawsuit in court.

If the context is new, Nielsen continues, the next question is whether “there are ‘special factors’ indicating that” courts are “at least arguably less equipped than Congress to ‘weigh the costs and benefits of allowing a damages action to proceed.’ ‘If there is even a single “reason to pause before applying Bivens in a new context,” a court may not recognize a Bivens remedy.’” Nielsen points to at least two “special factors” at play in this case that “‘suggest[] that Congress did [not] want’” prisoners to be able to recover monetary compensation from federal officials. First, he said, the Administrative Remedy Program – a grievance process created by the Bureau of Prisons in 1979 – “provides an alternative remedial structure for federal prisoners.” Second, he added, the Prison Litigation Reform Act, enacted in 1996, requires prisoners to use all available internal prison appeals like the ARP before going to court.

Nielsen further tells the justices that they should throw out not only Watanabe’s claim but Bivens along with it. Bivens, Davis, and Carlson, he says, “are relics of the bygone days when federal courts thought it appropriate to create causes of action congress had not authorized.”

The federal government sides with Nielsen to the extent that he argues that Watanabe’s claim should be thrown out. U.S. Solicitor General D. John Sauer tells the court that “Carlson has become entirely obsolete” in light of new laws like the PLRA and the creation of the ARP. “And given courts’ lack of authority and expertise in setting prison policy,” Sauer said, “courts will always have a reason to pause before extending Carlson to that new context. That means that no more Carlson claims will be viable.” But, Sauer continues, the court should decline Nielsen’s suggestion to “consider overruling Bivens in this case. If the Court wishes to consider that question,” Sauer writes, “it should do so in the context of a Fourth Amendment case that more closely resembles Bivens itself.”

In his brief, Watanabe insists that his case “arises in th[e] prototypical Carlson context.” “It is,” he writes, “a damages action against a federal prison officer who was deliberately indifferent to a federal prisoner’s serious medical needs in violation of the Eighth Amendment.” Moreover, he continues, both his claim and the claim in Carlson involved “the same kind of defendant in the same kind of federal institution under the same legal framework.” The fact that he suffered from a different medical problem than Carlson, or that the inmate in Carlson died while Watanabe survived does not make his case a “new context,” he argues. Instead, he contends, such differences “are case-specific, factual variations to which” Carlson’s “settled rule applies.”

Watanabe pushes back against Nielsen’s contention that, because he is still alive, he (unlike the inmate in Carlson) also has other remedies available to him. Only money, he says, can compensate him for “his past pain and suffering.”

Watanabe tells the justices that, contrary to Nielsen’s suggestion, Congress has indicated that claims like his and the one in Carlson should be allowed to go forward, albeit with some restrictions. He points first to the Westfall Act, a 1988 law that stripped prisoners of their ability to sue federal officers under state law for their injuries, but specifically carved out an exception for “federal constitutional claims like those in Carlson.” And although the Prison Litigation Reform Act requires prisoners to pursue all of their internal appeals “before pursuing a Carlson claim,” it nonetheless shows, Watanabe argues, that Congress intended for Carlson claims to be available.

Watanabe urges the justices to leave Carlson in place even if the court might reach a different decision today. Among other things, he argues, that decision “was not egregiously wrong” and Congress has relied on it in fashioning remedies for inmates – two factors that courts consider when deciding whether to overturn their prior cases. And more broadly, he contends, if the Supreme Court were to overrule Carlson, it “would create a novel situation, never contemplated by Congress, in which federal prisoners who are subjected to needless suffering in violation of the Eighth Amendment have no recourse against the responsible officers.”

A decision in the case is expected sometime next year.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

HECM broker rankings for August stay steady as shift to proprietary products rolls on

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

August HECM endorsements kept Atlantic Avenue Mortgage, loanDepot and Caliver Beach atop 12-month broker rankings. RMI data later showed September endorsements fell 6.7% to 1,790, the lowest since April 2020.  AI Summary

Home Equity Conversion Mortgage (HECM) endorsements for August show that Atlantic Avenue Mortgage, loanDepot and Caliver Beach Mortgage remain the top brokers on a 12-month rolling basis, according to a report published Wednesday by HECMWorld.com based on data compiled by Reverse Market Insight (RMI).

The three leading brokers have remained intact for much of 2026, with Atlantic Avenue now at a rolling total of 1,002 endorsements, down slightly from 1,009 in July. loanDepot endorsed 43 loans in August, upping its rolling total to 476, while Caliver Beach produced 19 loans, dropping its rolling total slightly to 352.

The rest of the top 10 on a rolling basis includes West Capital Lending (194), C2 Financial Corp. (191), Barrett Financial Group (155), NEXA Lending (140), Carrington Mortgage Services (122), Senior Lending Corp. (115) and Integrity 1st Mortgage (107).

West Capital Lending had a strong month and jumped ahead of C2 Financial for #4, 194 to 191, after adding 15 loans in August. Caliver Beach Mortgage stays at #3 with 352.

HECMWorld.com noted in an email that it’s still waiting for data from the Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) to compile its HECM direct lender rankings for October. The data for the prior month is typically released in the first few days of each month.

September data published by RMI showed that HECM endorsements were down 6.7% from August, with the 1,790 loans in September representing the lowest total for any month since April 2020.

Last month, New View Advisors released the newest edition of its Proprietary Reverse Mortgage Production Index, which shows that private-label products are driving overall growth for reverse mortgage originations even as HECM production wanes. The company said that prop loans represented more than 60% of reverse mortgage volume in September — a share that is likely to rise further.

“Rising interest rates may challenge the reverse mortgage industry in the fourth quarter, but proprietary loans could still propel reverse mortgage industry totals to over $8 billion in 2026, versus $7.3 billion in total new originations of reverse mortgages in 2025,” New View explained.

In his weekly “Ribler on Rates” video series, Dan Ribler of Longbridge Financial said Wednesday that the 10-year Treasury yield, which closely connects with long-term mortgage rates, has offered some relief to capital markets and borrowers in recent weeks.

“In the last 30 days, the 10-year Treasury yield is down about 35 basis points at the same time as the S&P 500 is down about 2.5% to 3%. This is not a structural, massive repricing of anything,” Ribler said.

“This is just a 30-day cycle of risk-off — currencies, dollars moving out of riskier assets, whether it’s stocks, whether it’s AI infrastructure, into safer assets and Treasurys. As more dollars move into Treasurys, the price goes up and the yield comes down, and we get a little bit of rate love on our rate sheets.”

HousingWire‘s Mortgage Rates Center on Wednesday showed that 30-year fixed rates averaged 7.34%, although rates for reverse mortgage products typically run higher than that figure. Higher rates impact reverse mortgages differently than traditional forward mortgages as they eat into available proceeds for borrowers.

As lenders look to keep business flowing through the end of the year and beyond, they’re looking to technology as a potential spark plug.

On Wednesday, Nationwide Equities Corp. announced the launch of ReverseReach, which aims to help traditional mortgage brokers with lead generation efforts through their databases of past clients.

“We created ReverseReach to make uncovering those highly lucrative opportunities simple, efficient and accessible — even for brokers who have never closed a reverse mortgage before — so they can confidently and comfortably begin originating them,” CEO Paul Lamparillo said.

“I’m Still Very, Very Bullish”: Charles Hoskinson Predicts Bitcoin Will Hit $250,000 in 24 Months

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Cardano (ADA) founder Charles Hoskinson has predicted that Bitcoin (BTC) will reach $250,000 within 24 months, maintaining a bullish outlook despite ongoing questions about cryptocurrency adoption and market volatility.

Speaking during a fireside chat with Ana Tutova, founder of AI Crypto Minds, at TOKEN2049 in Singapore, Hoskinson said he expects the crypto asset to climb substantially over the coming two years. He also argued that artificial intelligence could create new demand for cryptocurrencies as autonomous agents increasingly conduct economic activity.

“Yeah, I’m still very very bullish. You know, the next 24 months that Bitcoin will reach $250,000,” Hoskinson stated.

His forecast comes as Bitcoin faces questions about its near-term direction, with traders watching key price levels for signs of a recovery or a deeper correction.

Beyond Bitcoin’s price outlook, Hoskinson pointed to the growing relationship between artificial intelligence and blockchain technology as a potential source of demand across the crypto market. He argued that AI agents will need digital assets to transact, pay for services and participate in online marketplaces. Cryptocurrencies, particularly stablecoins, could provide the financial infrastructure for these activities.

“For agents to have agency and for agents to have economic identity has to be crypto,” he said.

Hoskinson believes AI-driven commerce could expand the practical uses of blockchain networks beyond speculative trading. He also suggested that combining crypto with AI could significantly increase transaction volumes across several digital assets.

“If we can get AI into crypto in just the right way, uh it’ll 10x or 100x the volume of a lot of these cryptocurrencies, and it’s going to be very good for our prices,” he said.

The Cardano founder added that the industry needs to prioritize real-world applications rather than relying primarily on price speculation.

“We have to move beyond speculation to utility,” he stated, identifying AI and real-world asset tokenization as areas with potential to deliver more practical blockchain use cases.

Despite his optimism, Hoskinson acknowledged that cryptocurrencies have yet to become part of everyday life for most consumers. He said the industry has made considerable progress in user numbers and market value, but the experience of using digital assets remains too complicated for widespread adoption.

He also highlighted privacy as a barrier to institutional adoption, noting that publicly visible transactions can expose sensitive financial information. Hoskinson expects the next stage of blockchain development to focus more heavily on usability, identity, privacy and interoperability. He believes improvements in these areas could help bring cryptocurrency into mainstream financial activity before the end of the decade.

Meanwhile, market indicators offer mixed signals about Bitcoin’s near-term trajectory. Santiment reported Tuesday that Bitcoin had lost approximately 30,000 non-empty wallets since October 16, bringing the total to around 59.95 million. The analytics platform suggested that smaller holders may be exiting after an extended period of stagnant price action, potentially creating conditions for a local market bottom.

The firm further identified $80,000 as a key level, with a recovery above roughly $82,500 strengthening its potential bottom scenario.

“Retail capitulation has historically been more constructive for price than a sudden flood of new buyers chasing rallies. Crypto typically moves opposite crowd expectations at emotional extremes, so shrinking participation while BTC struggles near $80K strengthens the case that a local bottom may be forming shortly.” It wrote.

CryptoQuant analyst ‘Trader Gemini’ also identified Bitcoin’s Trader Realized Price, then in the mid-$70,000 range, as a level to watch if prices fall. The metric reflects the average acquisition price of Bitcoin last moved between one and three months earlier. However, the analyst cautioned that the level is not a guaranteed floor, although it has historically acted as support during some bullish periods.

At press time, Bitcoin was trading at $80,550, reflecting a 4.14% surge in the past 24 hours.

Highest scoring centre-backs in Premier League history: Gabriel, Terry, Kompany…

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Gabriel Magalhaes has chipped in with several goals for Arsenal, but where does he rank among the highest-scoring centre-backs in Premier League history?

The Brazilian has made a name for himself as a goalscoring defender and has become particularly lethal from set-piece situations.

Given how dangerous Arsenal have become in that department, it’s no surprise that the 28-year-old has scored so many goals over the past six years.

In fact, since making his debut for Arsenal in 2020-21, he’s scored more goals (21), than any other Premier League defender.

The likes of Fabian Schar, Virgil van Dijk, Michael Keane, Kurt Zouma, John Stones, Ben Mee and Lewis Dunk have also scored double figures in that time, but none have scored more than Gabriel.

“That shows a lot and not only set-pieces strategy but also mentality,” Arsenal midfielder Mikel Merino told BBC MOTD when describing Gabriel’s goal against Newcastle back in September 2025.

“Other teams if they get six/seven corners and they don’t score, they start to lose belief in the qualities but for us it’s a matter of keep doing what we do, doing it at the highest level and in the end the last one, last second, we scored and I’m really happy.”

In the wider context of the all-time standings, the Brazilian defender is now joint 10th.

He’s now only one goal away from matching Laurent Koscielny’s Premier League tally and is only four goals away from securing a place in the top five.

Unsurprisingly, John Terry tops the list with a mammoth 41 league goals. Second on the list is Ian Marshall with 33 goals, although he did spend a portion of his career playing as a forward.

The rest of the top five is then made up of Virgil van Dijk, Gary Cahill and William Gallas.

Providing that Gabriel remains in the Premier League for the foreseeable future, there’s a chance that he could retire as the second-highest-scoring centre-back in the league’s history.

Admittedly, catching up to Terry’s record of 41 goals does seem out of reach, even for Gabriel.

Here’s the full breakdown of the highest-scoring centre-backs in Premier League history.

1. John Terry – 41 goals 2. Ian Marshall – 33 goals 3. Virgil van Dijk – 31 goals 4. Gary Cahill – 28 goals 5. William Gallas – 25 goals 6. Joleon Lescott – 24 goals =7. Matt Elliott – 22 goals =7. Laurent Koscielny – 22 goals =7. Sami Hyypia – 22 goals =10. Robert Huth – 21 goals =10. Craig Dawson – 21 goals =10. Gabriel Magalhaes – 21 goals 13. Sol Campbell – 20 goals =14. Michael Keane – 19 goals =14. Ugo Ehiogu – 19 goals =16. Fabian Schar – 18 goals =16. Nathan Ake – 18 goals =16. Vincent Kompany – 18 goals =16. Phil Jagielka – 18 goals =19. Frank Leboeuf – 17 goals =19. Gareth Southgate – 17 goals

JPMorgan Projects Bitcoin To Outperform Stocks And Gold With 10% Annual Return Over The Next Decade

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

JPMorgan sees Bitcoin outperforming US stocks and gold in the next decade. The prediction is notable, as the bank has historically been cautious on BTC.

In a report published today, JPMorgan Asset Management made a bold prediction: Bitcoin will outperform gold and US stocks over the next decade. This bullish projection from a global banking giant further legitimizes Bitcoin as a strong investment alternative to traditional safe-haven assets.

According to the new report, JPMorgan forecasts Bitcoin to outperform gold, US stocks, and bonds with 10% annualized returns over the next 10 to 15 years. The bank expects BTC to beat gold and stocks over the long term, giving investors new confidence in the cryptocurrency.

Despite Bitcoin’s high volatility, the institution expects BTC to deliver a 10% annual compound return over the next 10 to 15 years, outperforming real estate, gold, and US stocks.

In its 2027 Long-Term Capital Market Assumptions report today, October 20, JPMorgan predicts Bitcoin’s compound annual return at 10%. The report also estimates an arithmetic return of 20.9% and an annualized volatility of 55%.

The forecast outlook places Bitcoin ahead of many traditional investments in terms of compound annual return. The bank anticipates US large-cap stocks to return 7.5% yearly. It also expects gold to deliver a 5.5% annual return while projecting U.S. real estate investment trusts (REITs) to return 8.2% annually.

These projections suggest JPMorgan sees more robust long-term return potential in Bitcoin than in the other major asset classes mentioned above.

Despite the positive outlook, the bank sees higher volatility in Bitcoin, estimating BTC’s annualized volatility at 55% versus 17.2% for gold. This provides Bitcoin with opportunities for massive gains, but also substantial losses.

This is the first time that JPMorgan has included Bitcoin in its long-term return forecast in the 31-year history of its annual report. This shows a change of heart after years of criticism from the bank’s CEO.

Matt Hougan, Bitwise CIO, has celebrated JPMorgan’s Bitcoin forecast, terming it another milestone in the asset’s acceptance.

The development suggests that Bitcoin is becoming an important asset that JPMorgan doesn’t want to ignore. This shift highlights a wider change in attitude on Wall Street, including from JPMorgan’s CEO, Jamie Dimon, who has historically been skeptical of Bitcoin.
