The 10 Best Snowboard Boots of 2027

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

What to know: After testing 38 pairs of snowboard boots, our crew’s favorite all-mountain options are the Nitro Team Double BOA (men’s) and the ThirtyTwo Lashed Double BOA (women’s). If you’re looking for a comfortable, more affordable pick, it’s hard to beat the Nidecker Altai, which is available in men’s and women’s sizes. Heading into the backcountry? Check out the versatile ThirtyTwo Focus BOA x Sweetin or the skintrack-slaying K2 Waive.

Testing snowboards is one of our favorite things in the world. Testing snowboard boots? Not so much. Quite frankly, it’s hard to find a pair of snowboard boots that fit your anatomy and riding style (not to mention budget), and breaking in a new pair can be daunting even for seasoned snowboarders.

Luckily for you, our crew of diehard testers risked blisters and toe bang while putting 38 pairs to the test in the Rockies, Wasatch, and Sierra over the past couple seasons to bring you the best snowboard boots available today. Below, you’ll find our top picks for boarders on a budget, tried-and-true favorites for all-mountain riding, as well as powerful freeride boots and our go-to option for splitboarding.

Pros and Cons + Versatile for true all-mountain use + Comfy out of the box + Solid heel hold – Hard-chargers may crave a stiffer freeride boot – Fit can be snug

Nitro’s best-selling Team is an all-mountain menace that’s dependable from park to peak. This double BOA version pairs new H5 BOA coilers with Nitro’s fast-and-easy inner lace system, which kept our ankles locked in place for responsive turns and smooth transitions.

The main draw of the comfy-out-of-the-box Team is its versatility. A Tahoe tester who loves throwing down in spring parks as much as he does exploring Palisades hike-to terrain found the mid-stiff Nitro supplied the sweet spot of support and shock absorption. There’s enough lateral give for jib laps and tweaked airs, and the molded backstay provides stability on steeper descents. Meanwhile, the EVA foam liner, EVA midsole, and an airbag pocket in the heel make for velvety-smooth landings. Plus, the Vibram outsole was at home scrambling in the alpine. “Zooming around on melted rocks was totally chill,” reported that Tahoe tester.

These Nitro boots are best for intermediates and up, but heavier, hard-chargers may gravitate toward a stiffer boot in serious freeride terrain. Fit was on the snug side, so it’s smart to size up a half size. It also comes in a wide version.

Pros and Cons + Comfy out of the box + Lightweight + Good shock absorption – Flimsy insoles – Durability concerns around the foam outsole – Doesn’t pair with FASE bindings

The lightweight, affordable, and medium-flexing Nidecker Altai also happened to be one of the top rated snowboard boots in the comfort department. An Eastern Sierra tester with a history of foot pain was impressed by the coziness from lap one. “I haven’t had these professionally fit, and they are remarkably comfortable out of the box,” she reported, shouting out the plush, foot-hugging liner and the roominess of the 3D-molded toe box.

After replacing the “flimsy” stock insoles with custom footbeds, a snowboard instructor called the Altai “perfect for everyday riding” thanks to “a blend of comfort and responsive mid-flex.” She co-signed the double-BOA lacing and Velcro-cuffed liner that stayed tight and secure. The asymmetrical closure, which shifts BOA lacing inwards and over the flattest zone of the forefoot, is a hallmark of Nidecker boots, and the Swiss outfitter claims it reduces pressure and discomfort. Testers confirmed as much: “Stayed tight, no pressure points, super comfy,” said one.

By loosening the upper BOA, a freestyle-oriented rider could dip in and out of the park, boning tweaked grabs and buttering to his heart’s content. The lightweight, cushy foam outsole absorbed flat landings well, he reported, although multiple testers worried about the foam sole’s durability compared to harder-wearing rubber outsoles found on the pricier options in this guide. Additional dings? The lipped backstay doesn’t fit well with FASE bindings while unstrapping, and a couple of testers had to size up a half size.

Beyond that, our crew recommended the boot for lower intermediates to advanced riders, warning that newbies could benefit from a softer flex and hard-charging speed demons would crave more support.

Pros and Cons + All-mountain boot that loves park laps + Great cushioning and shock absorption – BOA needs frequent retightening – Beefy build

ThirtyTwo’s frills-free Lashed Double BOA walks the tightrope between power and playfulness. It gives all-mountain freestylers enough comfort for bell-to-bell riding, response for off-piste exploration, and lateral flex to flow through the park. “Cushy and comfortable,” said a longtime snowboard instructor who spends full days on her feet.

The dual-density, heat-moldable liner and light yet robust rubber outsole with integrated foam cushioning spared testers’ joints from impact. A classic dual-BOA design cinches down the tongue, and an internal ankle harness hugs the forefoot. Testers had mixed feedback on lacing: The inner lacing and BOAs work in harmony to dial fit, but we noticed some fraying of the eyelets and needed to continually tighten throughout the day. Additionally, the Lashed doesn’t have the most low-profile silhouette. But that didn’t bother our test crew too much, as the beefier build helps provide durability, warmth, and comfort. Our biggest complaint this year? One tester had a pressure point from the liner’s revamped, structured heel.

While the Lashed can be overkill for beginners in search of Sorel-level softness, after a brief break-in period, it mellows to a medium flex that’s well suited to resort ripping and freestyle applications.

Pros and Cons + Enough backbone for hard-charging + Versatile enough for all-mountain use + Comfortable and secure fit – Sloped toe caused some cap-style binding straps to slide off

One of K2’s stiffest women’s offerings, the Trance features a mid-firm backstay that speaks to all-mountain and freeride rippers. “There’s plenty of stability for freeriding, but enough flex for cruising on a pow day, too,” said a Tahoe all-mountain tester, who gave the Trance a Goldilocks flex rating: “Not too stiff, not too flexible—a great middle ground.”

We were major fans of the heat moldable and multi-density Intuition Control foam liner, as well as three-point liner lacing harness that locks it in place. One BOA dial controls the upper half of the boot, and another tightens the lower, while an articulating cuff allows the two halves of the boot to flex independently. The result? “No blisters or heel lift—my foot fits nicely, snug but not squished,” our tester said, which is especially high praise coming from someone with chronic ankle pain.

Another Tahoe rider with a wider foot agreed: “The BOA stays tight, with no pinch points, rubbing, or foot sleepiness.” She dug the lugged rubber outsole’s superior traction. However, we had one concern: Due to the sloped shape of the toe, certain binding straps designed to fit over the toe cap would slip off, no matter how much one tester tightened them.

Pros and Cons + Unbelievably adjustable + Top-tier responsiveness + Low profile – Freeriders may want a stiffer boot – May be too constricting for wide feet or high arches

Head isn’t the first to bring a triple-BOA snowboard boot to the market, but they’ve created the best integration of a dial in the liner, resulting in a perfect Cinderella fit. One BOA helps lock the heel in place, while the other two independently adjust the upper and lower shell. After heat-molding the liner, one tester was pleased to report “no pressure points, hot spots, or blisters.”

One of the biggest selling points of the PSI, though, was the asymmetrical shell, which is reinforced with aramid mesh (a key component of Kevlar) to provide a natural, intuitive all-mountain flex. “They work without thinking about them,” said a tester. The PSI earned additional kudos for a sleek, low-profile silhouette that one tester said felt as nimble as “ballerina point shoes”— a quality that was highlighted while riding and scrambling on the recycled rubber sole during a spring photo shoot.

We recommend the PSI for all-mountain riders and aggressive park rippers who like to go big in the jump line or pipe, but feel hardcore freeriders may want a touch more stiffness. Also, the liner construction and overall shape limit vertical space, making the PSI best suited to average-width and lower-volume feet. Those with wide feet or high arches may find it too constricting.

Pros and Cons + Stiff, high-performance responsiveness + Wide toe box + Durable – Stiffer than advertised – A test boot had a pressure point

Have you ever ordered “mild salsa” only to have a rogue jalapeño light your tongue on fire? That’s how we felt in the Salsa boot. While Bataleon scores the boot’s flex a 6 out of 10, we’d give it an 8 or 8.5 since testers felt Bataleon’s rating lowballed the Salsa’s stout, sturdy build and high-performance flex from the injected spine along the Achilles and a curved, firm tongue. “Highly responsive,” said a Colorado all-mountain rider. “Excellent boots for hard-charging when you want to turn on a dime.”

Our tester gave the adjustability of the double-BOA high marks, though he occasionally had to retighten the inner lacing system and found fit best for normal to medium-wide feet. “A real highlight of this boot for me is the squared-off, wide toe box, which allows my toes some breathing room laterally,” he reported. It earned additional points for its durability, showing little wear after a heavy spring. Same goes for a foam midsole that ate up landings and a Vibram outsole that handled Aspen bootpacks like a boss.

The Bataleon Salsa is available in both men’s and women’s sizes. On the men’s side, we were hyped. On the women’s side, a pressure point from the liner’s stitching made riding difficult. This was likely a one-off issue, seeing as it was in just the left boot, not the right. But it’s a good reminder that it’s always a smart idea to try boots on at your local shop or double-check return policies if you shop online.

Pros and Cons + Ultra-stiff and powerful freeride charger + Durable and long-lasting – Long break-in period – Overkill for most riders

Much like a lithe mama cheetah hell-bent on feeding her offspring, the Insano is a slim, aggressive hunter, and its prey of choice includes cliff drops and technical freeride faces. It’s also the most durable option in this guide, though breaking it in can take a week or two of hard riding.

“Insanely stiff,” signed off a hard-charging Tahoe tester. Despite its low-profile silhouette, the Insano’s TPU-heavy construction delivers power and responsiveness. Cold-resistant thermoplastic urethane bolsters the toe cap, heel counter, and steely yet smooth-flexing tongue. There’s even a TPU shank embedded into the Michelin-made, bootpack-ready outsole, stabilizing the foot and transmitting energy to your edges.  “Most aggro boots I’ve ever ridden,” applauded another tester.

The negatives are obvious: It requires significant break-in, and it’s overkill for 99.8 percent of readers (maybe more). But the 0.2 percent will appreciate the cushy multi-density foam liner and heel-locking BOA lacing on ambitious freeride missions.

Pros and Cons + Responsive charger + Removable flex stiffeners + Top-tier outsole with crampon-compatible heel welt – No walk mode for splitboarding – Pricey

Austen Sweetin’s pro model boot, the Sweetin Focus BOA is not a split-specific boot and it doesn’t have a true walk-mode. But this boot was “born to scramble,” said a Tahoe tester who claimed the molded toe cap “kicked steps like a soldier.” “If you travel frequently or just want one boot to do it all, this is hard to beat,” another tester said.

Zippered gaiters help protect against powder when bootpacking, and the relatively slim Michelin Fiberlite rubber outsoles with a carbon shank provides stability—ideal for riding between the resort and backcountry. The Focus relies exclusively on BOA lacing—not our favorite for bigger missions and multi-day trips, as field repairs are a nightmare. However, pop the upper BOA dial to loosen it for some skintrack mobility.

The Sweetin’s stout construction pairs a dual-density liner with removable stiffening inserts for “direct response on any turn initiation.” Testers praised the liner’s Velcro wrap for eliminating heel lift and recommended the boot for all-mountain riders and freeriders, calling “couloirs and big, aggressive lines” its bread and butter.

The boot’s “overbuilt” construction and exceptional durability are both pluses for backcountry riders. However, a gap between the liner and tongue near the upper calf compromised some of its responsiveness and made it incompatible with closely cropped pant legs. Still rocking the Shaun White skinnies? Keep looking.

Pros and Cons + Stellar walk mode for a soft boot + Great downhill performance + Burly outsole, reinforced toe, and heel welt for climbing – Expensive – Walk mode is poor compared to hardboots

K2’s unisex Waive is a standout on the uphill and downhill alike, providing the balance our backcountry-bound testers appreciate. Uphill, the BOA dial “frees up range of motion in the upper calf, delivering an unparalleled walk mode for a soft boot” according to a Tahoe tester. The Waive also boasts a burly, Vibram sole with a reinforced toe for bootpacking and a heel welt for semi-auto crampon compatibility—both must-have features for ridge scrambling and spring objectives.

Though hefty compared to some resort boots, the Waive is still fairly low-profile for the split-specific category. And on the descent, the Waive’s mid-stiff flex doesn’t feel too stiff or aggressive, especially once you loosen the strap and pop the BOA, testers said. “You can surf pow, no problem. But there’s plenty of performance when you batten down the hatches on top of a sketchy line,” reported that Tahoe tester, calling the Waive his ideal split boot for daily use and travel.

No real demerits here, although it’s worth noting that hardboots are much more efficient at skinning and sidehilling than soft boots. Also, the price is tough to swallow. But remember: splitboarding is cheaper than therapy!

Pros and Cons: + Great adjustability + Cushy feel – Ridiculously priced – Step On design not for most

The Waverange X Pro is a playful, comfortable, mid-soft-flexing, surf-inspired boot designed to be used exclusively with Step On bindings. (If you’re unfamiliar with the Step On system, all Step On boots feature two cleats on either side of the toe box and one connection point at the heel. These three locking points snap into place when you step onto a compatible binding.)

High-end snowboard boots trend stiffer and more responsive, speaking to more advanced all-mountain rippers and freeriders, but the Waverange X Pro is softer and more approachable, yet still packed with high-end features like triple BOA dials for adjusting fit of the upper boot, lower boot, and asymmetrical liner. “Having three BOAs really allowed fine-tuning the fit, and choosing where I want the boots to be more or less snug,” commented a Mammoth snowboard coach. Testers appreciated the grippiness of the winter-ready Vibram outsole, as well as the cushy shock absorption supplied by integrated polyurethane and gel inserts. “Felt like walking on a cloud,” said a tester.

On the descent, testers recommended the Waverange X Pro for “mellow cruiser laps,” and “playful riders” because the flex is too forgiving for aggressive riding. In pursuit of more response, multiple expert-level testers found themselves cranking the BOA aggressively, resulting in some ankle discomfort. Most testers found themselves missing their traditional strap setups, but grom-wrangling instructors and parents, beginners, and folks unable to bend down may appreciate the convenience of this Step On boot.

No piece of gear is more subjective to the rider than a pair of boots. Nothing will inform your decision-making like a visit to your local snowboard shop, as trying boots on in person is invaluable. Not to mention, boot-fitting, heat-molding, and aftermarket or custom insoles go a long way to ensure a comfortable, high-performance fit. Here are seven tried-and-true tips to make the process of shopping for snowboard boots as smooth as possible.

Shop for Boots First: If possible, shop for snowboard boots before boards and bindings. Your boot size influences both your binding size and acceptable board widths. Some boots have compact footprints, which means you’re less likely to have toe or heel drag on a narrower board. If you end up buying a boot with a bigger footprint, or you have a size 10.5 boot or higher, you may want to consider a mid-wide, wide, or volume-shifted board.

Stick to Brands You Know and Love: Every brand has a slightly different fit. Some are known for being wider or skinnier, or running bigger or smaller. If your last pair of boots fit well, consider repeating your experience with that specific brand, as chances are the fit hasn’t changed much over time. Many testers swear by the strategy of purchasing a newer version of the same boot model.

Go Wide: Many popular boot models are available in both standard sizes and wide sizes. If you have trouble squeezing into snowboard boots, or you already shop for wide sneakers or running shoes, you might want to consider a wide option.

Invest in Insoles: Even high-end snowboard boots often sport garbage insoles (as reported by a tester who fits boots for a local shop). One of the best ways to improve fit, comfort, and performance is with an aftermarket insole. We’re partial to Superfeet in most instances, but Remind Insoles aren’t a bad play if you’re looking for more shock absorption. If you really want to go the extra mile, get a custom pair from a bootfitter.

Put Your Faith in Bootfitters: There’s a misconception that only skiers need to get their boots fitted. Once you’ve had your snowboard boots professionally fit, however, you’ll never go back—especially if you have a history of foot pain. Ideally, schedule an appointment with a bootfitter who is also a snowboarder, as they’ll have a better idea of what problems you’ll face on the hill. Bootfitters can help you with everything from run-of-the-mill heat-molding to boot punches, heel-hold adjustments, liner replacements, and custom insoles.

Look for Demos, Snowboard Shops, and Return Policies: Every foot is different, so it’s worth attending demos at your local hill, where you can try boots from local shops, or visiting your local shop to try on boots in person. When you do make a purchase, support your local snowboard shop if you can. (There is a special place in hell for people who try on boots in a shop and then go home and buy them online.) If you prefer to shop online, buy from a reputable online retailer like Evo, REI, or Backcountry that has a flexible return policy.

When to Consider Step Ons: Our snowboard testers strongly prefer traditional snowboard boots to Step Ons. But if you’re battling injuries that make bending over tricky, or you are a parent or instructor wrangling groms, it can be worth considering this alternative boot-and-binding system.

This year, we sent snowboard boots to testers at their home mountains in the Wasatch, Rockies, and Sierra to put them on the chopping block all spring long. Long-term testing is crucial for picking the best snowboard boots, as break-in periods vary. A boot might be stellar out of the box, only to blow out in ten days. Or a boot might be stiff, uncomfortable, and difficult to break in for the first week, then turn into our favorite boot once it’s been out on the hill for a spell.

We encouraged testers to swap out insoles and get boots heat-molded and professionally fit—whatever they would normally do if purchasing a new pair of boots.

Once chairlifts stopped cranking and splitboarders hung up their skins for the season, we asked our test crew to fill out review forms, answering questions like: Would you purchase these boots? What rider would like these boots? Are the boots bulky or low-profile? What are your impressions on the inner and outer lacing systems?

Finally, I—test director Drew Zieff—sifted through the review forms, compared this year’s favorites to our previous, unchanged favorites from years past, and compiled these reviews on the best snowboard boots of 2027.

Drew Zieff is a Tahoe-based freelance writer and a lifelong snowboarder. Burdened with unnaturally long second toes and skinny calves and ankles, Zieff is no stranger to the boot-fitting chair (he’s a regular at Tahoe SportsHub), and he generally doesn’t trust stock insoles any more than he’ll sniff rental ones. In addition to directing Outside’s snowboard test, a role he’s handled since 2016, he directs Backcountry Magazine’s splitboard coverage and nerds out on snowboard gear and travel for Whitelines Snowboarding, REI, Gear Junkie, among others. He spends his winters testing gear in his backyard backcountry zones or up at Palisades when he’s not chasing stories and storms around the globe.

In a word, Kordell Black is a character. An esoteric ripper and veteran gear tester based in the Wasatch, Black has traveled the world with his splitboard in pursuit of powder and off-the-beaten-track adventures. He approaches big mountain lines with creative, freestyle energy, specializes in finding powder stashes overlooked by the Utah crowds, and thrashes boots into oblivion in a matter of weeks. He will also obliterate you in a flat ground game of SKATE.

Truckee’s own Jenna Shlachter is as brilliant on the hill as she is off it—which is saying something since she’s a Doctor of Psychology, after all. When she’s not on the clock, she’s usually on her snowboard, ripping everything from park to powder. Her trophy shelf needs regular reinforcement, as every year she seems to add banked slalom awards to her already impressive collection. Lucky for us, Shlachter is also an unabashed snowboard nerd, and she’s been a key contributor to our snowboard test for the last few seasons.

Get a Head Start on Your Spring Garden by Planting These Perennials Now

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

By Nina Malkin, Gretchen Heber, Updated by Teresa Odle

Published Nov 4, 2026 4:04 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

Perennials can live for years or even decades, beautifying your garden with very little care. Flowering perennials typically produce blooms by the second year, though some will burst with color the very first year. Spring-blooming perennials signal the coming of warmer weather and more colorful gardens.

However, fall, with its combination of still-warm soil and cooler, wetter weather, presents an ideal opportunity to encourage root growth in new spring perennials. So admire this list of perennials to plant in fall, pick your favorites, and take advantage of planting spring-blooming perennial flowers before winter strikes.

Planting perennials in the still-warm fall soil gives the plants a chance to get established underground, building a strong root system that will prepare them for vigorous aboveground growth—and flowering—come springtime. This helps lessen the risk of heat and water stress in spring. In addition, fall planting is beneficial in drought-prone areas. According to High Country Gardens, “as plants begin to go dormant in fall, and their energy shifts from top growth to root growth, they use much less water.”

With cooler fall soil and typically less windy conditions than spring, weather tends to stress young plants less in autumn. If you live where it’s still blisteringly hot, wait until temperatures cool down, but don’t wait so long that the plants have little to no chance to get somewhat established before a freeze hits.

Many perennial plants fare better with a fall planting and are better able to produce flowers in spring. Several of the plants on this list are herbaceous perennials, which typically die back to the ground in winter anyway, but remain alive underground and leaf out in spring, so they are well suited for fall planting. As with any new plant, don’t expect your fall-planted perennial to reach its mature size and full flowering in its first year, but give it some time to establish.

If you get impatient for posies as the winter drags on, put in hellebores now. Summer is too hot for them, so these are among the best perennials to plant in fall. Also known as Lenten rose, this is one of the first flowers to appear each year, often poking through the snow in late winter or early spring with big, bowl-shaped pink, yellow, or maroon blooms. Hardy hellebores do best in slightly shaded spots and neutral to alkaline soils with good drainage. Though deer and rabbits avoid munching on hellebores, the plants’ leaves, stems, and sap are poisonous if ingested.

Best For: Early blooming in a mass planting or ground cover.USDA hardiness zones: 5 to 8Size: 12 to 15 inches tallLight: Dappled sunlight to partial shade

Certainly no shrinking violet, pasque flower is a showstopper with purple bell-shaped blossoms that is native to dry meadows in parts of Europe. Pasque is the Old French word for “Easter,” and this perennial is bound to bloom in time for the holiday (fortunately, it is also rabbit-resistant). The gorgeous garden star, which is also known as windflower and meadow anemone, flourishes in fertile soil with good drainage for easy purple spring flowers. Plant it in August if possible to give it time to produce blooms.

Best For: Winter to early spring color in cooler zones.USDA hardiness zones: 4 to 8Size: 4 to 10 inches tallLight: Partial shade to full sun

For colorful ground cover, consider creeping phlox. Phlox is the Greek word for “fire,” and this mat-forming plant will set your landscape ablaze with small, flat, fragrant blooms in purple, pink, red, or white beginning in March. It’s a favorite for filling in rock gardens and can be especially pretty draping over a wall. Choose a spot that receives dappled sun and has sandy soil that drains well. Though phlox might need dividing if it gets too crowded, it is a low-maintenance ground cover.

Best For: Colorful spring-blooming groundcover or to drape over walls or rocks.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 9Size: 6 inches tallLight: Dappled shade to full sun

Gardeners who don’t like a lot of toil may love growing Virginia bluebells. Plant them in fall and In early spring, the perennial pops out flowers that start pale and turn a rich, true blue. They thrive in moist soil and mostly shady conditions, eventually establishing a colony of pollinator-friendly ground cover filled with foolproof flowers that require virtually zero care.

Best For: True blue color in moist woodland gardens. USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 9Size: 1.5 to 2 feet tallLight: Partial to full shade

Prized for its graceful, nodding blooms, columbine is a woodland perennial, so it likes a partly shaded location and consistently moist soil. Its white, yellow, red, or blue flowers appear in early spring and bloom through midsummer. These native flowers also are deer- and rabbit-resistant, a plus in gardens visited by wildlife. The only caveat is that columbine is one of the shorter-lived perennials; if you love them, add a few plants every year or so to keep their color going and the butterflies and hummingbirds happy in mountain, forested, and cooler growing zones.

Best For: Spring color in natural and woodland gardens or meadows.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 8Size: 1 to 3 feet tallLight: Partial shade to full sun

Tall, resilient false indigo (also known as blue wild indigo) flowers in late spring through fall with thick stalks and spires of small dark-blue blooms. If blue isn’t your bag, check out hybrids in other colors, including yellow and pink. False indigo likes full sun but can deal with some shade and is best planted in fall in hotter climates. The drought-tolerant native that’s a member of the pea family beckons bees and butterflies, and is unlikely to get nibbled by deer and rabbits.

Best For: Blue flowers above oval blue-green leaves in drought-tolerant gardens USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 9Size: 3 to 4 feet high and wideLight: Partial shade to full sun

It must be those long, fuzzy, pale-colored flowers that give Astilbe its common nickname: false goat’s beard. This practically trouble-free plant that blooms from spring through summer is especially popular as a shady border plant and for its deer resistance. Its flowers contrast well against broad, leafy foliage. Astilbe thrives in loamy, slightly acidic soil and usually needs dividing every 3 to 4 years.

Best For: Border plant for part shade in gardens with acidic soil.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 8Size: 1 to 4 feet tallLight: Shade to part shade

Attention gardeners in the Southwest who can’t get by on cactus alone: Hartweg’s or Texas sundrops are a perfect perennial pick, flowering in March through October. Also called evening primrose, this night-blooming perennial features flowers with bright yellow petals that open around sunset and remain through the next day. Once classified as Oenothera, the low-growing plant thrives in regions with hot prairies and plains. Sow seeds or plant in partly shaded areas for earlier opening times.

Best For: Large yellow flowers that bloom in the evening for rock gardens or moon gardens.USDA hardiness zones: 5 to 9Size: 1 to 1.5 feet high and 2 feet wideLight: Part afternoon shade to full sun

Shade happens—and that’s not a problem for lamium. This lovely yet tough ground cover can easily take on bare spots like those beneath trees. Lamium boasts variegated foliage in silver, gold, and green along with dainty pink and purple flowers that appear in late spring and summer. This rugged plant can thrive in clay and alkaline soils, and it actually prefers dry conditions. Deadhead the flowers once they fade to encourage a new crop of blooms.

Best For: Ground cover under trees or other areas where sun transitions to shade.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 8Size: Less than 12 inches high with 2- to 3-foot spreadLight: Partial to full shade

For a flowering perennial that smells as nice as it looks, let dianthus adorn your landscape. Some dianthus species are biennial (living about 2 years) and they require fall planting so they can gain energy over winter. All types offer clusters of spunky spring-through-summer blooms in a range of hues, including pink, white, yellow, and red against blue-green leaves. Dianthus loves sun and well-draining soil, and is ideal for rock gardens, containers, and borders. Its rich nectar will attract pollinators, yet deer and bunnies won’t bother with it.

Best For: Sun-loving flower for rock gardens, containers, and along rock walls. USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 8Size: 6 to 12 inches high; 6 to 24 inches wideLight: Full sun

This cold-hardy daisy can add a touch of elegance to borders and garden beds. In late spring and early summer, the daisy comes alive with large white flowers with yellow centers, adding a contrast to other perennial garden flowers. The flowers fare best in rich soil with good drainage and are among perennial flowers to plant in fall (or sow from seeds). Shasta daisy is a flower that can handle heat. And while it does well with regular watering, it handles short periods of drought better than soggy soil. Deadhead spent flowers to keep the blooms coming, and even cut fresh ones for flower arrangements.

Best For: Sunny butterfly or cutting gardens or in containers. USDA hardiness zones: 4 to 9Size: 1 to 4 feet high; 1 to 2 feet wideLight: Full sun

Add a pincushion plant to your garden and enjoy the springtime reward of delicate lavender, pink, or white flowers borne on gangly stalks that rise from a clump of gray-green leaves. Perennial in all but the coldest regions, pincushion flower is moderately drought-tolerant, attracts butterflies and hummingbirds, and thrives in sunny spots of the garden. The cut flowers of this plant make a wonderful, somewhat quirky, addition to a bouquet. Sow the seeds in fall.

Best For: Rock gardens or sunny fairy gardens and along walkways.USDA hardiness zones: 5 to 9Size: About 1 foot high and wideLight: Full sun

A springtime profusion of plum-colored blooms greets those who plant purple coneflower or sow its seeds thickly in fall. The large, daisy-like flowers sit high above the medium-green foliage on this clumping plant. This hardy perennial can take a bit of shade but needs some sun to produce its stunning blooms. Purple coneflower is not terribly picky about soil, but will do best in soil that drains well. It’s also fairly drought-tolerant once established, and wilts a bit when it needs water. Harvest the blooms to add to indoor flower arrangements.

Best For: Native and butterfly gardens or meadows, or behind lower-growing flowers.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 8Size: 3 to 4 feet high; 1 to 2 feet wideLight: Full sun but can handle partial shade

Occasionally referred to as tickseed, and recently reclassified as Anacis, this pretty flowering plant is most commonly known as coreopsis. It comes in both annual and perennial varieties. If you’re planting in the fall, be sure to look for a perennial variety. The profuse bloomer produces scads of yellow flowers throughout spring and summer, and even into autumn. Deadhead spent flowers to encourage this long bloom time. Coreopsis is drought-tolerant and deer-resistant, but it will attract pollinators and songbirds.

Best For: Natural areas in rocky or sandy soil, rock gardens, and butterfly gardens.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 9Size: 3 to 4 feet tall; 1 to 2 feet wideLight: Full sun or light shade

The delicate, intricate flowers of Oenothera lindheimeri (also known as gaura or whirling butterflies), are said to resemble supercharged butterflies. With white-tipped pink “wings” that surround long white stamens topped with yet more pink, gaura provides an elegant feature in the garden. This loose, shrubby plant is native to Texas, Louisiana, and Mexico, and it’s drought- and heat-resistant.

Best For: Native or prairie-style gardens or areas with sandy, well-draining soil.USDA hardiness zones: 5 to 9Size: Flower stems to 4 or 5 feet; about 2 feet wideLight: Full sun

This member of the mint family offers both attractive purple flowers for bees in late spring and aromatic foliage is considered an herb that can go into teas or savory dishes. It’s a reliable plant that can stand alone in a rock garden or spread to form a border along a path. Catmint needs no fertilizer and will rebloom if you shear the plants to remove the first bloom of flowers. Plant it in fall in time to establish some roots before frost. It prefers good drainage and can thrive in rocky or sandy soil. It’s also drought-tolerant and deer-resistant.

Best For: Borders and walkways or in pollinator rock gardens.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 8Size: 1 to 3 feet high, 1 to 2 feet wideLight: Full sun

Add bleeding heart to your garden in fall, and come springtime it will bring arching stems bearing delicate 1- to 2-inch-long heart-shaped pink flowers. An herbaceous, bushy plant formerly known as Dicentra, bleeding heart has clumps of foliage that can go dormant in heat while flower stalks rise above it. Plant this one in part shade, in moist, well-draining soil with plenty of organic material. As summer wears on, bleeding heart will likely retreat, but it will be back on display after winter subsides.

Best For: Woodland gardens, interplanted with foliage ground covers.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 9Size: 2 to 3 feet high and about 2 feet wideLight: Partial to full shade

A sprawling, almost vine-like shrub, lantana decorates hot landscapes in late spring and summer with masses of delicate flower clusters in purple, orange, pink, yellow, red, or white. It is drought-tolerant once established and although it loves heat, it can feature in summer annual flower beds or containers in slightly cooler climates. Depending on where it grows, lantana can become shrub size. Butterflies flock to this hardy plant, which is deer-, pest-, and disease-resistant. Bear in mind that lantana is toxic to livestock, cats, and dogs.

Best For: Hot areas and drought-tolerant gardens to attract butterflies and hummingbirds.USDA hardiness zones: 7 to 11Size: 1 to 6 feet high; 3 to 5 feet wide where perennialLight: Full sun

Yarrow blooms from spring to fall with masses of umbrella-shaped flower heads gracing the tops of tall stems bearing fern-like foliage. Popular as a cut flower, yarrow is also beautiful in the landscape. You’ll find yarrow in a rainbow of colors, including yellow, pink, red, white, and gold. It’s drought- and heat-tolerant, but yarrow seeds need a cool period to germinate, so it’s best to sow seeds in the fall. Yarrow can spread aggressively, so plant it in a place where you’d like to see a sea of color.

Best For: Naturalized areas like meadows and drought-tolerant butterfly gardens.USDA hardiness zones: 3 to 9Size: 1 to 3 feet high and wideLight: Full sun

Many spurge types offer early spring color, and cushion spurge is no exception. Its small yellow flowers are surrounded by yellow/pale green bracts, creating the impression of a larger flower. The mounded plant can grow in poor soil like sand or in loamy soil. Although the plant thrives in sun, it might need a bit of afternoon shade in hot climates. It’s drought-, deer-, and pest-resistant, and easy to grow and maintain. However, its milky sap is slightly toxic and can cause itching on contact.

Best For: Small spaces in rock or cottage gardens, containers.USDA hardiness zones: 4 to 8Size: 1½ feet high and wideLight: Full sun with some partial shade

Create the feeling of your own “poppy field” or Georgia O’Keefe painting with oriental poppies. Though the flower petals are paperlike, they are large and full of interest around the center. Look for varieties in classic deep red, orange, coral, or even white. Sow seeds or plant nursery transplants about a month before your first expected frost to aim for blooms in late spring to early summer. Note that these flowers do best in cooler and drier regions of the country and require soil that drains well.

Best For: Asian or cottage garden mixed in with late summer bloomersUSDA hardiness zones: 3 to 7Size: 3 feet high and nearly 2 feet wideLight: Dappled to full sunlight

Gardeners in warm regions might want to consider planting Mexican bush sage or other salvias in fall to establish good root systems before spring. In its northern ranges, this big beauty might go all the way to the ground in winter, but it will come roaring back in spring. Mexican bush sage forms an ever-expanding clump of stalks bearing long leaves and velvety purple flower spikes. It thrives in sunny, hot locations and is drought-tolerant once established.

Best For: Borders, mass plantings, or butterfly gardens, especially on slopes.USDA hardiness zones: 7 to 10Size: 3 feet high and 3 feet wideLight: Partial shade to full sun

Spring is typically the time that plants are plentiful from nurseries, and that’s usually when many gardeners are ready to improve their landscapes. But there are garden centers and online sellers that have stock through fall (and even end-of-season sales) or seeds for fall sowing. Keep these tips in mind for fall planting:

The earlier you can plant spring-blooming perennials in fall, the better. This typically is 6 weeks before the average first frost, so you can often plant perennials in September. This gives plants warm soil with cool nights and time to set roots, which can help prevent frost heave. This happens when thawing of frozen ground pushes plants up out of the soil. If some roots have taken hold and you have mulched the ground, the plant has a better shot. However, avoid planting on hot days or less than a month before frost is expected.

Although rule is a strong word for this principle and doesn’t apply to all flowering perennials, knowing about the “3 years” can help gardeners care for their new plants with realistic expectations about how they will look.  • The first year, most perennials need to grow roots, so they might not show much growth above the ground or flower as advertised; give them extra water to help the roots establish.  • In their second season, plants begin to grow a little more but modestly and might display more flowers.  • By the third year, perennials begin to shine and flower as expected; they also might need pruning.

Yes, fall is a good time to plant many, but not all, perennials. One reason is that you have lived with your landscape for a summer and might be ready to add some color for the next year. Most of all, the combination of still-warm soil but cooler nights helps transplants ease into their new home without the stress of heat or cooler spring soil so the roots have a chance to establish for better spring health. Just be aware of your average first frost date and be sure to count backward when adding spring-flowering plants in fall.

It’s best to wait and plant bare-root plants in late winter to early spring while they still are dormant, not in fall. This can include roses and other shrubs or fruiting trees. Perennial bulbs to plant in fall will bloom in spring, but wait until spring to plant summer-flowering bulbs. Some flowering plants are less hardy to cold and freeze than others. It is best to avoid planting those in fall, especially if you are on the low edge of their hardiness zone. If you live in a cold, damp area, you might want to wait until spring to transplant any flowering perennial subject to root rot, which can occur when roots sit in moist, cool soil.

All You Need to Care for Your Lawn & Garden

Keeping your grass green and your plants thriving doesn’t just take a green thumb—it starts with the right tools and supplies.

Nina Malkin is a published novelist and magazine veteran who has been contributing to BobVila.com since 2016.

Gretchen Heber is a writer, marketer, and entrepreneur based in Texas. A former garden editor and designer with the Austin American-Statesman and Lexington Herald, she is passionate about succulents, herbs, and fruit trees, as well as lighting, energy efficiency, and all things home.

Teresa Odle is a writer and editor who has lived and gardened throughout the mountains of arid New Mexico. She joined the BobVila.com team as a freelancer in March 2020, editing and writing helpful articles mostly on gardening and outdoor living.

NASA Shares Plans to Accelerate Artemis, Return to Moon

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

In an update on Oct. 23, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman and other agency leaders shared the agency’s path to returning Americans to the lunar surface in 2028.

Agency leadership provided updates on the previously announced acceleration plans for the Artemis program, advancing toward the agency’s Artemis III test flight in 2027 and Artemis IV Moon landing mission in 2028. Building on the priorities  announced at Ignition earlier this year, NASA leadership shared the agency’s significant progress across its missions, working with industry partners to test critical capabilities, reduce risk, and build toward a sustained American presence on the Moon.

“This is a very energized NASA, and these updates are only a small part of that story. We are bringing extreme focus to the mission to ensure delivery of world-changing outcomes as quickly, safely, affordably, and successfully as possible,” said Isaacman. “NASA is driving outcomes to make Moon missions a reality, and we’re not waiting for others to bring their plans to us — the campaign must move with urgency as strategic competitors pursue a permanent presence beyond Earth.”

The agency shared details of the Artemis III mission, which will test rendezvous, docking, and operations between NASA’s Orion spacecraft and lunar lander test vehicles from SpaceX and Blue Origin in Earth orbit. Four astronauts will fly aboard Orion, launched on NASA’s SLS (Space Launch System) rocket, for a planned 14-day mission in Earth orbit to reduce risk ahead of the Artemis IV moon landing mission.

The Artemis III mission will unfold in sequence: Blue Origin will launch a Blue Moon lander test article into Earth orbit, SLS will launch Orion and its crew, docking with the Blue Moon lander test article, then SpaceX will launch a Version 3 Starship lander test article while Orion will undock from Blue Moon and dock with Starship.

The Oct. 23 updates follow other recent updates about the Artemis missions. Hardware for Artemis III is on track, with processing timelines sharply reduced by about half. NASA also redesigned, built, and tested a critical fuel-line component in-house in five months, at roughly a tenth of the cost of an earlier outside-vendor estimate.

“NASA should be expected to do this kind of work, and we have some of the best technical knowledge in the world on rocket hardware and operations to do it,” said Jeremy Parsons, the agency’s Artemis program manager. “It’s an example of how we are rebuilding our core competencies and developing centers of excellence in technical functions instead of continuing to outsource expertise.”

For the agency’s Artemis IV mission in 2028, astronauts will launch aboard Orion on the SLS rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida. NASA is working with SpaceX and Blue Origin to develop lunar landers to carry those astronauts to the Moon’s surface. Both companies have redesigned their approaches to simplify the missions and make them more efficient and flexible as compared to previous mission designs.

SpaceX’s Starship will refuel in Earth orbit, rendezvous with Orion, and then carry the spacecraft toward the Moon. Blue Origin’s approach involves transfer stages in Earth orbit to push its Blue Moon Mark 2 lander toward the Moon, where it will meet Orion in a closer lunar orbit before descending to the surface.

Both SpaceX and Blue Origin are targeting to be able to support a crewed lunar landing in 2028.

For Artemis IV, NASA is pursuing two upper-stage options for the SLS rocket: its current stage, the interim cryogenic propulsion stage; and a stretched version of ULA’s Centaur V which can carry more propellant. Pursuing both options ensures NASA is maintaining flexibility and preventing single-point dependency.

NASA also announced plans with spacesuit provider Axiom Space to develop a second, lighter suit design called the “Sortie suit” to enable the first moonwalks. The Sortie suit will not replace Axiom’s primary suit, which will be used for longer, more demanding missions in the future. The Sortie suit is roughly half the weight of the larger suit and lets NASA move faster on the earliest surface missions.

As part of a Golden Age of innovation and exploration, NASA will send Artemis astronauts on increasingly difficult missions at the lunar South Pole to explore more of the Moon than ever before. In addition to providing economic benefits and advancing scientific discovery through its Moon missions, NASA will establish an enduring human presence on the lunar surface and build on the agency’s foundation for the first crewed mission to Mars.Learn more about Artemis:

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

Cult Phone-Maker Nothing’s Mystery Screenless AI Device Is Coming Next Month

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Nothing CEO Carl Pei believes your work tools can be cool too. He's building one to prove it.

In the introduction to the essay published on X by Nothing CEO Carl Pei on Wednesday, it almost sounds like he’s arguing against the premise of standalone AI devices.

In Pei’s post, he suggests AI’s “iPhone moment” is still several years away. This could read as though Nothing is holding its horses rather than diving into the fray right now. But that isn’t the case; in fact, it’s quite the opposite.

“Our first AI-native product ships next month,” Pei says. “It’s a tool, but one built with the personality and care that have come to define a Nothing product. It has no new screen, and it couldn’t have been an app.”

Pei is playing it characteristically coy. He’s dropping clues, and you can bet there will be more to come in the ensuing days and weeks up until the official launch of this mystery product.

Standalone AI devices are a burgeoning industry, even if they’re yet to result in a hugely compelling, must-have consumer product. Whether it be pins, pendants, glasses, headphones or tabletop devices, tech companies are flooding the market with new native AI devices. Some companies, such as OpenAI, are working on ideas behind closed doors, whereas others, such as AI note-taking experts Plaud, are already establishing a strong and loyal customer base.

British startup Nothing is predominantly known for its retro-inspired audio products and cool smartphones, around which the company is building a cult following as an alternative to more mainstream, generic phone companies. It’s more closely aligned with lifestyle and fashion brands than most of its tech brethren. In spite of this, it sounds like its first AI device will be more work than play.

Just like computers, AI will need to prove itself in the realm of productivity — the workplace — before it spills over into being truly useful and appreciated in our personal lives, writes Pei. And so, Nothing is building a tool for the workplace, albeit one with the company’s customary design-forward approach.

“We were taught that seriousness and beauty could not share a room,” says Pei. But he wants you to know that work tools can be beautiful, and he promises Nothing’s will be.

This is all very mysterious — perhaps more so because the image accompanying Pei’s post actually looks like it could be a snapshot of one of many different smartphones from the past decade or more. The simple black rectangle with one rounded corner looks distinctly unlike a Nothing device, and something a less imaginative tech company might produce.

We shouldn’t be fooled by this, though. Nothing has yet to bore us with a design, and it seems unlikely to start now — especially after Pei’s treatise on the subject. No doubt there will be more hints forthcoming, as Nothing gears up to persuade you that its products belong just as much in your office as at a warehouse rave in Berlin, and that its approach to AI is essential to your workflow above and beyond the offerings of its strait-laced competitors.

12 forgotten ballers now playing in the Qatar Stars League: Mitrovic, Verratti…

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Footballers moving to the Middle East has become part and parcel of the modern game, but it’s usually the ones going to Saudi Arabia you hear more about.

Qatar, after hosting the 2022 World Cup, isn’t sitting back when it comes to investing in some big footballing names either.

Familiar names like Roberto Firmino and Riyad Mahrez have ended up there, along with a fair few players you probably haven’t thought about for longer. Like these.

Serie A enthusiasts will remember a time when Piatek was all the rage. For a couple of months, at least.

Talk about a good first impression. A four-goal haul on his debut in the Coppa Italia before scoring nine goals in his first seven Serie A games for Genoa. Wowsers.

A mid-season step up to AC Milan followed and Piatek made a reasonable start there too, before taking the cursed number nine shirt for his second season and never quite being the same.

Piatek went on to play for Hertha Berlin, Fiorentina and Salernitana before finding his shooting boots again during a two-year stint with Istanbul Basaksehir.

He moved from there to Al Duhail in 2025, scoring nine league goals in his first season in Qatar.

A World Cup winner with Germany in 2014, Draxler went on to become a four-time Ligue 1 winner with PSG, plus a Primeira Liga winner with Benfica.

He left Europe behind in 2023 to move to Al Ahli SC, a club based in Doha (not to be confused with the Saudi Pro League side where Ivan Toney plays).

The attacking midfielder scored six goals from 11 games in his first season in Qatar before 12 from 18 in his second and seven from 20 in his third.

It’s been a decent start to the season for the 33-year-old this time too, with three goals from eight games so far.

A new entrant into the Qatar Stars League this summer, Bennacer joined Al-Gharafa from AC Milan.

The one-time Arsenal man had spent the last 18 months of his time with Milan out on loan, first at Marseille and then at Dinamo Zagreb, before his contract got terminated.

A month later, he signed for Al-Gharafa on a two-year deal. But he got sent off on his debut for the club in the Asian Champions League against Saudi side Al Hilal.

A move from Saudi Arabia to Qatar has become fairly common for ageing players who were once shiny new toys in the Saudi Pro League but become surplus to requirements.

It happened to Firmino, it happened to Mahrez and it happened to Brozovic too.

Best known for his eight-year spell with Inter and as part of the Croatia sides that excelled in the 2018 and 2022 World Cups, Brozovic spent three years with Al-Nassr before a September switch to Al Sadd.

The midfielder was only able to make one appearance for his new club before being sidelined with a groin injury.

A popular player in his time at Porto, Brahimi has been playing in Qatar since he left the Portuguese giants in 2019.

The winger first spent three years with Al-Rayyan before joining current employers Al-Gharafa in 2022.

He’s captained them in most of their games this season, scoring five goals from eight games in the league.

Widely considered the best Italian player to never play in Serie A, Verratti spent his peak years winning trophies galore in France with PSG – a club, of course, with Qatari owners.

When he reached the end of his time in Paris, he was conveniently sold to Al-Arabi for €45m.

Two years later, the midfielder made a move across Doha to join rivals Al Duhail, where he still plays now.

Joselu had his moment in the sun back with Real Madrid in the 2023-24 season, but that was all he got there.

He was swiftly sold to Al-Gharafa for a two-year stint there, before embarking on a new challenge in the same league in October with a free transfer to Al-Sailiya.

The former Stoke and Newcastle striker scored on just his second appearance for his new club.

The less said about his season in the Premier League with Aston Villa the better, but Veretout went on to have a decent career in Serie A and Ligue 1, playing for clubs like Fiorentina, Roma and Marseille.

In 2025, the midfielder left Lyon for Al-Arabi, where he scored twice from 22 Qatar Stars League appearances in his debut season.

He has one goal to his name this season – the last covered by his contract as things stand – as well.

His Liverpool days are very much forgotten but Alberto was a more than decent baller for Lazio.

After eight years in Serie A, he left them in 2024 to join Al Duhail, before making a cross-Qatari transfer two years later to Al-Wakrah.

Former Leeds striker Rodrigo is now onto his third Qatari club. First he joined Al-Rayyan in 2023, before having a loan spell with Al-Gharafa in 2024-25.

This summer he signed for Lusail, newly promoted to the Qatar Stars League.

He’s done well so far with five goals from his first eight league games with the club.

Sarabia arrived at Wolves in 2023 with a decent-looking CV, including spells with Sevilla and PSG after coming through the Real Madrid ranks.

The midfielder left Wolves when his contract expired in 2025 and signed for Al-Arabi.

He enjoyed a decent debut season in Qatar with nine league goals from 21 games.

It was a bit of a shame to see Mitrovic leave the Premier League for Saudi Arabia in 2023. He’d done pretty well for Fulham and probably still had something to give at a good level.

But after his two years with Al Hilal came to an end, he didn’t head back to Europe. Instead, he stuck in the Middle East with Al Rayyan.

He scored five goals from 11 games in an injury-hit debut season in the Qatar Stars League.

Irish Designers Share Their 7 Coziest Style Secrets That We Should All Be Stealing

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Here’s how to embrace that “Green Wave” with your interiors.

Over the past few years, Americans have become notably charmed by Irish culture—even the L.A. Times coined it a “Green Wave.” From splitting the “G” when drinking a pint of Guinness and bragging about it on social media to spotlighting Irish celebrities like author Sally Rooney, musician Hozier, and actress Saoirse Ronan, we as a society—myself very much included—have become a bit obsessed with the Emerald Isle, and I think it’s high time we let this Green Wave roll into the interior design scene.

If you’ve ever had the privilege to visit a home in the Emerald Isle, you’ll know that Irish spaces are warm, welcoming, and lived-in, with many of the design elements specifically used to prioritize comfort and community, something American homeowners have begun adopting. “Irish homes have traditionally needed to be comfortable, functional, and adaptable to a climate that can be cold, damp, and unpredictable,” Galway-based designer Niamh McKenna tells House Beautiful. “Traditionally, our homes are also much smaller, so beautiful rooms must also work for everyday life.”

However, it’s not just the environment which influences their interiors. Keep reading to find out the quintessentially Irish design details that local designers love to celebrate and believe American homes should borrow.

During the Georgian era, which spanned from the early 1700s to the early 1800s, Ireland experienced a population boom. During this time, Dublin also underwent a sizable redevelopment when it comes to architecture and urban infrastructure, according to History Ireland. It’s these elegant details that designer Julianne Kelly loves so much and thinks others should adopt.

“There is so much history in these houses and the proportions are beautiful: high ceilings, ornate cornicing, original sash windows, fireplaces, and rich paneled doors,” Kelly says. “These details give a room a sense of history and permanence.”

The Merrion Square area in Dublin, she adds, is a great place to walk around to view some of these rich elements in person, specifically the iconic painted Georgian doors.

Once you leave the streets of Georgian Dublin, you’ll find the sprawling countryside with “a beautiful, varied landscape that is impossible to ignore when designing the interiors for a house,” Kelly explains. She uses many natural elements found just outside the window in her designs, like greenery, stone, timber, and natural fibers like wool and linen that feel authentic to the country and only improve with age. Colors inspired by the scenes outside help the interior feel connected to the earth as well.

The textiles specifically add much-needed warmth and coziness to a room. “I love these as they add personality, a sense of calm, and an old worldliness that doesn’t look over manufactured or brand-spanking new!”

“The fireplace has traditionally been at the heart of the Irish home, and I think that idea is still incredibly relevant,” Kelly says. Ornate or simple, stone or tiled, wood-burning or gas—the details of the fireplace don’t really matter, as long as it serves as a natural focal point and the furniture is arranged around it.

McKenna adds that centering the hearth rather than the TV promotes conversation and connection. “It’s a reminder that a room can be designed around how people gather and interact,” she explains. The energy and warmth of a fireplace, even as modern homes move away from open fires, brings comfort and beauty to any room, and that’s something worth preserving.

Step into an Irish person’s home, and you’ll likely find a lot of old mixed in with the new. “Irish people love to hold onto their items,” Kelly says. “I’m not sure if it’s due to our famine history, but on most projects I work on, people have sentimental items that I have to work with.”

The designer will always try to work with these existing pieces to come up with some sort of eclectic mix of modern and vintage. She loves how unique and personal these homes turn out to be; they tell a great story. “And the Irish love a good story,” she adds.

In Ireland’s rainy climate, a home’s interiors—especially practical, hardworking spaces—need to feel both beautiful and comfortable.

“Bathrooms and kitchens are among the hardest-working parts of our homes, and we see it important to give the most functional part of our homes the same thought and attention as our living spaces,” McKenna explains. “We love to connect the indoors to the outdoors in these spaces, by using natural materials, earthy colors, and organic textures to create an interior that is as beautiful to experience as it is practical to use.”

Much of the residential architecture in Ireland is designed to work with the chilly, wet climate. “Traditional Irish architecture features deep window reveals, thick walls, and smaller windows than what might be seen elsewhere,” McKenna explains.

These choices could easily make a home feel dark and cold, but the designer says that when oriented correctly to the outside light, these elements only add to a cozy, warm environment in the winter and a cool place for relief in the summer. “I love that these characteristics also create a depth, shadow, and texture that are difficult to replicate with decorative finishes alone,” she adds.

Similarly to the architecture, the interiors are decorated to account for the damp climate, as well. “We make comfort a priority rather than a finishing touch, by layering furnishings such as window treatments, tactile rugs and cushions, and blankets,” McKenna says. Even if someone were to decorate their home rather minimally, the layers of tactile fabrics found in Irish homes still ensure the space feels inviting and lived-in.

Canva's mass layoffs are a warning sign for creative tech

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Artists and creatives are familiar with the fear of AI. Illustrators and graphic designers have watched in dismay as increasing numbers of businesses decide that AI slop is good enough for their branding and marketing needs. And many put the blame on companies like Canva and Adobe, who have been aggressively rolling out AI tools in their software.

Canva's just revealed another side of the story: the tech companies driving the change aren't immune to its impact. The Australian design software giant has announced the biggest restructuring in its 12 years of history, with plans to eliminate or relocate more than 1,200 jobs. That's almost a quarter of its global workforce, and it will include staff who helped to build the platform.

Canva aims to “collapse layers” in its organisation and bring “more teams together in person”. It's handling the layoffs in the classiest way possible, promising six months of salary and 12 months of healthcare to those affected, alongside career support, a fund for those who want to start their own business – and it's letting people keep their MacBooks.

But the layoffs highlight an inescapable contradiction. Even those responsible for the ubiquity of AI image generation are suffering from its expansion. Rather than a push for growth and innovation, Adobe and Canva's rollout of AI tools feels more their own desperate fight for survival, and it's a fight they might not be able to win.

"Success in our first chapter doesn’t guarantee success in the next," Canva CEO Melanie Perkins writes in an update to staff. "This has been our hardest year yet, and as we shared earlier this year, our growth has slowed for the first time in a decade."

There's been talk of an “SaaS-pocalypse” as AI becomes advanced enough to make specialised software redundant. We've already seen the release of an open-source vibe-coded Adobe alternative, and it works to an extent. Generating your own software is only going to get easier, as is asking an AI bot to generate design assets directly.

Despite its heavy investment in AI, Adobe's stock price has fallen by around 25% in the past year and over 60% in the past five years. It's not clear that even AI leaders like OpenAI and Anthropic, the makers of ChatGPT and Claude, can become profitable as Chinese rivals emerge delivering similar technology at a cheaper price.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Some creatives opposed to AI might feel a twinge of satisfaction at seeing a prominent proponent floundering. But Canva has also been a positive force, even if you don't use it's AI tools. As well as its premium platform, it has one of the best free Adobe alternatives in its free Affinity app, providing much-needed competition.

Creatives using the app might have cause to worry about how long it will be offered as a loss leader. Canva's shown a strong commitment to it so far, recently adding new raw editing tools and an iPad app. But its recent decision to pull the previously free Remove.bg into its paid suite shows that the structure and availability of creative software is constantly in flux as developers try to work out how to respond to competition from AI.

A veteran news writer and photographer, he now works as a project manager at the London and Buenos Aires-based design, production and branding agency Hermana Creatives. There he manages a team of designers, photographers and video editors who specialise in producing visual content and design assets for the hospitality sector. He also dances Argentine tango.

Arsenal manager Mikel Arteta has blocked two outgoings from North London this January.

That's according to new reports that claim the champions are having to fend off interest from European giants for some of their biggest stars who have started the season slowly.

With the Gunners now top of the league and in a strong position to go deep in the Champions League, it appears that Arteta wants to keep the squad that won him the Premier League last term.

It may only just be November, but Arsenal midfielder Martin Zubimendi has been linked with an Emirates Stadium exit this January since the transfer window last closed.

The 2026 World Cup winner signed for Arteta last summer ahead of the likes of Liverpool, Real Madrid and Barcelona in a huge coup for the club, but has already found himself fourth in the pecking order in midfield, following the emergence of Myles Lewis-Skelly as an option in the engine room and the signing of Bruno Guimaraes over the summer.

Gabriel Martinelli, Gabriel Jesus and Leandro Trossard all left before the season began, with just Christos Tzolis replacing the trio and Eberechi Eze given more of a chance to play on the left wing.

BA's Best Orecchiette With Sausage and Broccoli Rabe

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Unlike many iconic Italian recipes, this luxurious union of silky broccoli rabe, chile-flecked sausage, garlic, and curvaceous pasta is a weeknight hero. You can make it start to finish, in about 30 minutes. The key to successful execution is to go way beyond blanching when it comes to precooking the broccoli rabe in boiling salted water. For tender rabe that melds seamlessly with pasta and sausage, start by separating the thick stems from the rest of the stalks and giving them a head start before adding the rest. It removes any unpleasant bitterness and also means the rabe will cook into the saucy base of browned sausage and sweated garlic in just minutes.

While orecchiette is the traditional pasta choice, any short pasta will work. Sausage has become a popular addition to the true original Pugliese dish of stewed broccoli rabe with pasta, but a tin of anchovies makes a great substitute for added oomph and intensity.

This recipe is one of BA’s Best, our vault of the Test Kitchen's definitive takes on iconic dishes.

Cut 2 large bunches broccoli rabe, trimmed, in half crosswise to separate thick stalks from thinner stems and florets. Cook thicker stalks in a medium pot of boiling salted water until crisp-tender, about 5 minutes. Add thinner stems and florets and cook until all of the broccoli rabe is tender (the tip of a paring knife should slide through the stalks with little to no resistance), about 3 minutes. Transfer to a rimmed baking sheet with tongs; let sit until cool enough to handle (no need to squeeze out excess water unless dripping wet). Coarsely chop into bite-size pieces.

Meanwhile, cook 1 lb. orecchiette (little ear-shaped pasta) or other short pasta in a second medium pot of boiling salted water until al dente (or use same pot with fresh salted water). Drain, reserving 2 cups pasta cooking liquid.

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a medium Dutch oven or other heavy pot over medium-high. Cook 1 lb. hot Italian sausage, casings removed if needed, breaking up with a wooden spoon, until golden brown and nearly cooked through, about 5 minutes. Add 1 head of garlic, cloves separated, peeled, thinly sliced, and remaining 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil. Cook, breaking up sausage even finer if needed and stirring often, until garlic is golden brown and softened, 3–4 minutes. Add broccoli rabe and cook, stirring occasionally, until very tender and wilted, about 5 minutes.

Add 4 Tbsp. (½ stick) unsalted butter, cut into pieces, pasta, and 1 cup pasta cooking liquid. Cook, stirring often and adding more pasta cooking liquid as needed, until pasta is coated in a clingy sauce. Taste and add some crushed red pepper flakes if desired. Add 1 oz. Parmesan, finely grated (about ½ cup), and stir, adding more pasta cooking liquid a splash at a time as needed to keep sauce smooth and shiny, until incorporated; season with salt.

Divide pasta among shallow bowls and top with more Parmesan.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/amwell-boosts-2026-guidance-after-strong-q3-boasts-government-business" hreflang="en">Amwell bets AI boom will boost demand for its unified care platform</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →
Amwell bets AI boom will boost demand for its unified care platform" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

As health plans and employers contend with a growing array of virtual care and AI-enabled tools, Amwell is positioning its digital health platform as the infrastructure layer for AI-enabled care.

Executives at Amwell, formerly known as American Well, touted the company's platform strategy, government contracts and higher-value clinical services as the main drivers of future growth during the company's third-quarter earnings call Tuesday.

Rising healthcare costs are pushing health plans and employers to adopt tech-enabled care solutions, but they are looking for measurable engagement and outcomes from digital health vendors, not just access, according to Ido Schoenberg, M.D., Amwell chairperson and CEO.

The proliferation of point solutions, accelerated by AI, is creating more vendor sprawl and member confusion, Schoenberg told investors and analysts during the call.

There's also a lack of proof with many digital health tools, he noted. "It is hard to see which programs work and harder still to replace one without disrupting members. That sprawl is accelerating. AI has made it faster and cheaper to build a clinical program. Investors have backed hundreds of new digital health companies this year, many of them built on AI. Our customers feel pressure to offer these programs, but each one brings its own cost, its own integration, and its own front door. The opportunity is real. So is the complexity," he said.

More patients are turning to AI for health guidance, but those interactions don’t necessarily lead them to appropriate, covered care, creating the risk of poor clinical decisions and unnecessary costs for health plans, Schoenberg said.

"This is the problem Amwell is determined to solve for our customers. We help them improve clinical and financial outcomes. We do it through activation, getting more of their members into technology-enabled care in place of conventional care that often costs more. We give our customers one platform under their brand with one integration. It provides reliable, scalable rails to bring in a program, exchange data with it, and tests whether it works. The member's experience stays consistent, simple, and engaging. A common data structure proves the outcomes and improves performance over time," he said.

As AI adoption grows, a unified care platform becomes more valuable, Schoenberg asserted.

"We are not selling an AI feature. We are the trusted platform where AI-powered care runs with clinicians in the loop," he said during the Q3 call.

Amwell's customers represent more than 90 million covered lives, and the company also serves 9.6 million beneficiaries of the Military Health System.

Amwell's platform can help drive member engagement and utilization of digital health tools, executives said.

"Most of the health plans we talk to still see engagement in the single digits. We address that with always-on engagement, outreach to members through our customers' own channels within the rules they set. When a customer switches it fully on, members use the platform at more than three times the rate we see when it's off," Schoenberg said.

The platform runs on a common data structure, he noted. "It sends a member into a program with the right information. It collects outcomes back, and it reports value in the customer's own terms, whether that customer is a health plan or an employer that the plan serves. That is how a program proves its return on investment and how it gets better over time," he said.

In response to a question from an analyst, Schoenberg suggested pricing for the solution could evolve from fixed Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) fees toward outcomes-based arrangements in which Amwell shares in the savings it produces for health plans.

Amwell executives view that platform strategy as key to the company's growth.

In Q3, Amwell reported revenue that was slightly above Wall Street's consensus and a narrower adjusted loss than analysts expected. Amwell executives also reiterated confidence in reaching positive operating cash flow and breakeven to positive adjusted EBITDA in the fourth quarter.

Amwell brought in $49 million in Q3 revenue, according to financial results published Tuesday afternoon. Revenue was down approximately 12.9% year-over-year, but $1 million above the high end of management's guidance range, according to Chief Financial Officer Mark Hirschhorn.

Subscription revenue was $23.9 million, down approximately 22.5% year-over-year and 6.8% sequentially, Hirschhorn said. “The year-over-year decline continues to reflect the previously disclosed churn we've discussed on prior earnings calls,” he added.

Amwell Medical Group (AMG) visit revenue was $23.7 million, up approximately 11.7% year over year, while AMG paid visits totaled approximately 301,000 visits, essentially flat year over year, Hirschhorn said.

Virtual primary care visits were up 41% year over year, continuing that segment's strong growth trajectory. Total platform visits were approximately 760,000 visits, down approximately 30.5% year over year—though Hirschhorn noted the decline is consistent with previously discussed portfolio changes.

The telehealth platform reported a net loss of $11.7 million during the quarter, compared to a loss of $29.3 million for the same period a year ago, Hirschhorn said. Adjusted EBITDA loss narrowed to $3.6 million from a loss of $12.7 million a year earlier.

Amwell executives are bullish on revenue growth from its own clinical services, including virtual primary care and behavioral health.

Executives also highlighted the company's government business as it delivered solid third-quarter financial performance. Amwell announced in September that the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) issued a letter of intent to deploy its platform enterprise-wide.

"I want to be precise about what this is," Schoenberg said. "The letter is not a binding contract. A definitive agreement still has to be negotiated and executed under federal procurement rules, and that work is underway."

Schoenberg noted the letter followed a "comprehensive evaluation."

"It cited scalable video consultations, interoperability, cybersecurity compliance and support for integrated care delivery," he said. "In plain terms, the capabilities the department evaluated are the ones we already run at scale for the military health system, and the department's own scale is considerable."

Amwell executives are optimistic the company will sign a contract with the VA "over the next couple of months," Hirschhorn said, with implementation beginning in 2027, but no definitive agreement exists yet.

Moreover, Schoenberg said the platform is continuing to negotiate a "direct, longer-term contract" with the Defense Health Agency (DHA).

The DHA, which oversees services for the Military Health System, renewed its contract with the telehealth platform in August 2025. In June, it called for sole contracts with Amwell and four other vendors.

DHA discontinued its payment for behavioral health and automated care services with Amwell last year. Schoenberg said the cessation was due to the Department of Defense’s cost-cutting measures—not the department’s satisfaction with the services.

Hirschhorn said that Amwell is raising its full-year revenue guidance to between $204 million to $206 million, up from $195 million to $205 million. The company now expects full-year adjusted EBITDA to be a loss in the range of $6.9 million to $7.9 million, a meaningful improvement from its prior range of a loss of $12 million to $16 million.

Amwell forecasts full-year AMG visits between 1.32 million to 1.37 million visits.

“In summary, during the third quarter, we exceeded our revenue guidance with stronger visit volumes, a stable subscription base, and a leaner cost structure that positions us to finish the year on a strong note,” Hirschhorn said.
